The 4 Best Super Automatic Espresso Machines

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These robot baristas will do every little step for you—except latte art.

Here’s something I think someone out there needs to hear: Not everything should be easy. Not every problem should be outsourced to the robots. Oh, ChatGPT can make a podcast about your kids’ days. That’s a shortcut that leads you nowhere. One thing, however, can be totally handed over to the robots with no consequence. At-home coffee. You can make espresso a hobby, turn it into rocket science—some on our Esquire staff do just that. But most of us just want our morning latte or Americano, a caffeine fix to get us going. If that’s you, the best gift you can get yourself is a fully automatic espresso machine—or, as it’s known by its more fun name, a super automatic espresso machine.

I love my Breville grinder (it makes the task quick work every time), but some folks just want simplicity and a latte on demand. Can’t say I blame them. An onboard hopper and grinder is one of the boxes a super automatic espresso machine must check. The others? A digital screen that lets you select beverages, adjustable settings and user profiles, and, most importantly, it must do all the weighing and measuring for you, ensuring it brews the same drink every time. Many, but not all, come with milk frothers and tanks to hold your milk or milk alternative.

At their best, the process is as easy as filling it up with beans and milk and then letting the warm-up rinse cycle boot. After that, any hot coffee or espresso drink you want (and sometimes cold-brewed ones too) is available at the push of a button. Some add extra steps, but the core of it is no measuring, no tamping, no wasted time. And not a single wasted cup. From De’Longhi, KitchenAid, and Terra Kaffe, these are the best super automatic espresso machines at every price point that will brew you a consistent espresso every time.

If you’ve read about products on Esquire for any amount of time, you know that we stan De’Longhi. My editor Luke Guillory and I can attest: When we see Magnifica, we say “magnificent.” For the price, the Magnifica Duo is both an excellent value and also everything you really need in an automatic coffee machine. It comes with a milk tank equipped with a foaming wand and dispenser. That whole apparatus is completely dishwasher safe so you don’t have to worry about scrubbing a thing. You are buying an automatic espresso machine after all.

It doesn’t have an LCD touch screen, but the touch-sensitive buttons pair with a small screen to make drink selection take a matter of seconds. It can be a bit clunky at first, but getting used to it doesn’t take much time. From there, you can program quick selects to get straight to your favorite drinks in even less time. The machine cleans itself and, eventually, it will pay for itself too.

For me, the most important thing an espresso machine can automate is maintenance. I’m always forgetting to clean my coffee makers (among the dozens of other gadgets I need to keep clean), but I never have to worry about that with my KitchenAid. This super automatic espresso machine and iced coffee maker rinses the machine and milk tube routinely, and in the settings I can always see how close a part is to needing a cleaning. On top of that, it’s a super easy machine that makes tasty beverages. It doesn’t have the 50 plus drinks of a $2,000 De’Longhi, but you have most of your basics including cappuccinos, lattes, and even iced coffee and iced espresso.

The KF3 is the least expensive of KitchenAid’s line of super automatics because it doesn’t come with a milk tank. Instead, the AutoMilk system it does have is brilliant. Just stick the provided milk tube into any vessel and it will extract the proper amount of liquid into the dispenser tank above your cup, where it is steamed and frothed to the ideal temp. After your drink is ready, you’ll be prompted (but not forced!) to run a rinse of the milk tube. I recommend it; keep that stuff clean.

And finally, it’s worth mentioning this machine is made from PFAS-free parts. This means a much reduced risk to exposure to microplastics in your coffee. Everyone can appreciate that.

If having something that looks great on your counter while it does the bean grinding and shot pulling for you is the priority, you have to take a look at Terra Kaffe’s machines. The sleek, minimalist design says “retro future” without the rest of your kitchen needing to fit any specific vibe. You use a stainless steel dial to literally “dial in” your brew strength and temperature. It’s the perfect machine for those who drink a daily espresso or Americano.

I mention those drinks because, as any coffee drinker will know, they don’t contain milk. While it’s coming in at a nice price, the Demi does admittedly lack a milk foaming option. If you already own a milk frother at home, though, this might be a way to save some money and still get a fantastic, no-work espresso machine.

Here’s the tea. This is an older model—2023 is ancient by gadget standards—and they are getting more difficult to find in stock. At least this means the price has gone down... a little bit. The fact remains, every one of the coffee freaks on Reddit swears by the Eletta Explore. It is still the pinnacle of what De’Longhi’s super automatic machines can do, with dozens and dozens of hot and cold drink recipes at your very fingertips. The milk frothing tech is top notch and pretty much all of the parts are dishwasher safe for cleaning. It even includes a travel mug for those mornings when time isn’t on your side.

The only complaint I can see raised against it is that, at this price, many competitors have swappable bean hoppers. The $1,300 De’Longhi Rivelia has a bean swap system, where you can switch between two different bean hoppers—decaf drinkers love this for switching over in the afternoon. Users report mixed results since you still have to clear an unseeable number of beans from the grinder before the switch is complete. This can land you in a no-man’s-land where you might be drinking half-caf on accident. It’s why I’m still recommending the Eletta over that machine.

Automakers Want Congress to Permanently Ban Chinese Cars From the U.S.

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The Alliance for Automotive Innovation, which represents dozens of car companies and other industry businesses, said vehicles made by Chinese brands carry ​surveillance risks.

Chinese vehicles have become a serious political issue in the United States in recent months, and earlier this week, the Alliance for Automotive Innovation—a lobbying group that represents a wide range of automakers from General Motors and Toyota to Ferrari and Porsche—weighed in on the hot-button issue. The president and CEO of the Alliance for Automotive Innovation, John Bozzella, said that Chinese vehicles should be permanently banned from the U.S. market, citing factors such as security concerns and unfair business practices.

“We need to protect the U.S. market from a flood of Chinese imports that carry surveillance risk and disadvantage current manufacturers that have made generational investments in ⁠American communities,” Bozzella said, according to Reuters.

Support for banning Chinese vehicles is a surprisingly bipartisan issue, as Senators Elissa Slotkin (D-MI) and Bernie Moreno (R-OH) co-authored the Connected Vehicle Security Act of 2026 earlier this year. The bill unanimously passed the Senate Commerce Committee this summer, and is expected to be voted on next week during the short “lame duck” legislative session ‌after the midterm elections. A version of the Connected Vehicle Security Act of 2026 in the House of Representatives has now amassed over 100 cosponsors, too.

But it isn’t just security concerns that have legacy automakers pushing for an outright ban on Chinese cars. Bozzella cited significant gains from those vehicles in the European market as further evidence that regulations are necessary, claiming that the European Union imported more than 1.1 million vehicles from China in the first eight months of 2026—more Chinese cars than the EU imported in all of 2025. The Volkswagen Group, one of the companies represented by the Alliance for Automotive Innovation, cited competition from Chinese automakers as part of its major restructuring earlier this year.

“Given the obvious national security risk and China’s pattern of unfair behavior, Congress should protect our defense and advanced manufacturing base and stop Chinese automakers from doing here what they’ve gotten away ‌with ⁠in Europe,” Bozzella said.

The first wave of regulations on Chinese vehicles came during President Joe Biden’s administration. The original rule essentially banned Chinese automakers from selling or building passenger vehicles in America over concerns about data privacy. (Polestar will be banned from selling vehicles in the U.S. starting in 2027 as a result of these regulations.) But the Connected Vehicle Security Act of 2026 goes one step further, according to Senator Slotkin.

“I think it's an issue of economic security and national security," Slotkin told Road & Track in April. "You don't have to be a deep expert to understand that the Chinese Communist Party heavily subsidizes their industry, so they have a whole strategy of basically stealing our intellectual property—making stuff like cars and drones off of stuff that we've invented, and then heavily subsidizing them so that they undercut and kill the industry that they enter. They have a long history of doing that with drones, [and] the solar industry is ninety percent Chinese because of this very strategy."

Congressional aides and industry experts said there may not be enough time to approve the Chinese vehicle ban during this session if Democrats win control of Congress, according to Reuters. Even so, as momentum builds in both the House and Senate, legacy automakers remain hopeful. A 100-percent tariff on Chinese electric vehicles also remains in place.

A New York transplant hailing from the Pacific Northwest, Emmet White has a passion for anything that goes: cars, bicycles, planes, and motorcycles. After learning to ride at 17, Emmet worked in the motorcycle industry before joining Autoweek in 2022 and Road & Track in 2024. The woes of alternate side parking have kept his fleet moderate, with a 2014 Volkswagen Jetta GLI and a BMW 318i E30 street parked in his Queens community.

Google Is Forcing Schools to Choose Between Its Search Engine and AI

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Experts weigh in on the promises and pitfalls of Google’s new policy, which leaves schools unable to use Search unless they opt into its AI tools.

Google will automatically turn off its AI products for K-12 student accounts, requiring school administrators to manually opt in before students can use the technology, the company announced in a blog post on Tuesday. But there’s a big catch: If schools choose not to enable AI, students won’t be able to access Google Search.

In August, the company’s AI products were automatically enabled in Google Classroom, Google’s free learning management system for students of all ages. Tuesday’s announcement reverses that decision.

In October, tech and child-safety organizations wrote an open letter to 150 US school districts and their leadership, requesting that districts disable Google Gemini AI on school devices, concluding that “children are at risk of being harmed.”

“After listening to schools, teachers, leading researchers and policymakers, we are now updating our approach to controls for Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom and Google Search,” wrote Chris Phillips, Google’s vice president and general manager of education.

For student accounts with Google Search enabled, Gemini will feature a new Guided Learning study tool. The interactive feature helps students solve homework problems step by step rather than providing direct answers. Later this month, at select US schools, Guided Learning will launch as a pilot program to collect feedback before rolling out to more schools.

According to a 2024 Google blog post, over 150 million teachers and students worldwide use Google Classroom and Assignments, the company’s assignment distribution and grading app.

A 2025 New York Times survey sent to the 20 largest US school districts found that Google’s Chromebooks, which start at $179, are the most common device in classrooms, with about 80% of teachers reporting that their school district provided Chromebooks for students.

The default search engine on Chromebooks is Google Search.

New York City Public Schools, the largest US public school district, is a Google client. When asked for comment, a NYC Public Schools spokesperson pointed to NYC Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s X post.

“Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools,” Mamdani wrote in the Tuesday post. “No corporation should have the power to dictate how we teach our children. Our public schools exist to serve students, not to grow the profit margins of Big Tech.”

The second largest US district, the Los Angeles Unified School District, is also a Google client.

A Los Angeles Unified spokesperson told CNET in an email that the organization is reviewing Google’s announcement and its potential implications for schools, educators and students. The district believes emerging technologies, including AI, have the potential to enhance teaching and learning when introduced thoughtfully, responsibly and with clear safeguards, the spokesperson said.

“Our Emerging Technologies Framework establishes a set of principles to guide these decisions, including ensuring that technology supports rather than replaces educators, directly advances student learning, promotes equitable access, protects student privacy and security and is implemented transparently,” the spokesperson explained.

Ying Xu, an assistant professor at the Harvard Graduate School of Education and founder and director of the Harvard Child-Centered AI Lab, told CNET in an email that Google’s Guided Learning tool could buffer some of the negative consequences of using general-purpose AI tools.

“But to me, the deeper question is: Do students still need to learn how to search for information using traditional search engines?” Xu said.

In her work, Xu found that seeking information using traditional search engines, as opposed to AI models, involves different processes, ranging from how students formulate queries to how they evaluate information to how they integrate it from multiple sources.

“I’m a bit concerned that, even with Guided Learning, students may be losing opportunities to practice these important information-seeking skills,” said Xu. “One concern I have with Google’s announcement is whether schools will still have the option to support traditional information seeking without AI features.”

Until recently, the conversation focused on whether AI should be allowed in schools at all. Now, Google’s announcement is forcing schools to decide which specific AI features to use and under what conditions, without the support needed to make these decisions.

“I do think there’s a risk of widening inequalities here, as schools with more resources and technical expertise may be better positioned to navigate these decisions, while others may have fewer options,” Xu said.

However, she doesn’t think that moving away from Google entirely is necessarily the solution, especially for districts that already rely on its systems.

Instead, Xu thinks the type of support available to schools as they navigate AI-related decisions will be crucial.

“With or without Google’s decision about search functionality, the responsibility falls on schools to protect students’ right to learn from human teachers and peers in human-led classrooms,” said Jennifer L. Steele, a professor at American University’s Baker School of Education. “Tools like Gemini Guided Learning, which uses AI to walk students through difficult questions, are not the answer.”

When schools and teachers rely on tech companies to teach students how to use their AI tools and understand their strengths and weaknesses, that’s when problems occur, she said.

“If schools take that responsibility seriously, then how Google configures its apps won’t really matter,” Steele said.

When asked for a response to the backlash surrounding the decision to turn off its search engine for schools that reject its AI, a Google spokesperson pointed to the company’s X post stating that schools can choose to turn on Gemini, Gemini Notebook or Search individually.

However, that post doesn’t address the fact that AI Mode and AI Overviews will still be enabled alongside Google Search. So if schools don’t want AI enabled for students at all, they will still lose the ability to use the search engine.

A Google spokesperson didn’t immediately respond to a request for further comment.

Tracy Pizzo Frey, a former Google managing director, wrote on LinkedIn that Google could allow schools to turn off AI Overviews, but instead is choosing to take search away from students.

Search engine DuckDuckGo, for instance, has noai.duckduckgo.com, its AI-free search engine that automatically disables all AI features and filters out AI-generated images.

“AI overviews is a search module like maps, flights or weather,” a DuckDuckGo spokesperson said in an email. “Google can determine when it shows up and when it doesn’t.”

Experts caution that educators can’t count on tech platforms to police themselves. Because AI tools are now permanent fixtures, schools must shape their own path forward.

The primary hurdle is an uneven playing field, where wealthier districts have the resources to adapt, leaving underfunded systems struggling to keep up.

Fat Fender Garage’s GT/FX Pairs Sporty Looks with an 800-HP V-8

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

This radical truck is based on a 1956 Ford F-100 but swaps in a new chassis, an 800-hp Coyote V-8, and a modernized cabin with leather bucket seats.

Ford has produced a diverse array of vehicles in its 123-year history, from workhorses like the bestselling F-series trucks to supercars like the GT supercar. Now, a small outfit seeks to blend the philosophies of those two vehicles. Fat Fender Garage, a restoration shop based in Arizona, has revealed the GT/FX at SEMA, taking the body of a 1956 F-100 truck and giving it performance chops meant to emulate the capabilities of the Ford GT.

The GT/FX’s steel body was drastically reshaped by Fat Fender Garage, with a flatter hood and roof and wider front and rear fenders giving it a more aggressive stance. The custom running boards blend neatly into the rear fenders, while a chin splitter up front and a rear diffuser swiped from a Mustang GT350 give the GT/FX a track-ready look.

See all results for used Ford for sale near 31545

The bed now features distinctive flared edges, and the tailgate drops down with the push of a button. The side-view mirrors are fashioned from carbon fiber, and the gray paint job is completed with a black racing stripe just offset to the left. The only thing left untouched was the grille, maintaining a tie-back to the original F-100.

Behind that grille sits a Gen 3 Ford Coyote 5.0-liter V-8 with a 3.0-liter Whipple supercharger strapped on top, pushing out 800 horsepower and 750 pound-feet of torque. The hearty engine is paired with Ford’s 10R80 10-speed automatic transmission, which routes that muscle to HRE wheels wearing Toyo Proxes Sport 2 tires, measuring a beefy 315/35R-20 at the rear. Stopping duties are handled by Wilwood disc brakes with six-piston front calipers and four-piston rear calipers.

The cabin features modern-looking bucket seats; a modern screen and buttons are also part of the transformation, but they’re discreetly integrated. Plus, there’s a concealed climate-control system that blows air through a hidden vent, and there’s also interior LED lighting and a modern audio system.

The GT/FX showcases the breadth of Fat Fender Garage’s abilities, from paint to interior trim and exhaust setups. The truck even rides on the company’s own FF1 Performance Coilover chassis, which is designed to provide sporty handling despite the vintage truck’s body on top. Between the bodywork, the cabin, the powertrain, and the chassis, Fat Fender Garage went all in on transforming the F-100 from a utilitarian hauler into a sport truck that blends classic styling with modern power.

10 Action-Adventure Games That Are Unexpected Masterpieces

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Ronald is a video game journalist with more than 10 years of experience in the industry, working on several of the most important Spanish websites.

The action-adventure genre is one of the broadest in the entire video game industry, encompassing countless experiences ranging from stealth and hack-and-slash to horror and beat 'em ups.

Thanks to this broad definition, countless masterpieces fall under its umbrella, including many that, unfortunately, haven't achieved the popularity they deserve due to fierce competition.

Even within the realm of major blockbusters, not all underrated titles are obscure indie games—there are games that have fallen just short of mainstream success, though I'm here to help rectify that.

So, in order to shed light on some lesser-known campaigns that haven't yet cemented their undeniable excellence in the collective consciousness, I invite you to explore this list of ten action-adventure games that are unexpected masterpieces.

As the least recognized work from Tango Gameworks, a studio that has certainly gifted us with numerous magnificent journeys, Ghostwire: Tokyo stands out among the games with the greatest disparity between its quality and its fame.

Boasting one of the finest atmospheres crafted this decade, wandering the streets of Shibuya, soaring across rooftops, and taking down the most terrifying yokai imaginable is, by far, one of my highlights of the decade.

Whether in stealth or direct magical combat, tackling one of its turbulent side quests or hunting for collectibles, the game strikes an outstanding balance between making each mechanic satisfying while still maintaining a constant sense of terror in its setting.

As far as first-person games go, Ghostwire: Tokyo is part of the most immersive, rewarding, and distinctive I've played this generation, which is saying something considering the titans with which it shares category.

As the years have gone by, games that adhere to the Metroidvania philosophy have become one of my greatest obsessions, which inevitably led me to fall in love with Pipistrello and the Cursed Yoyo.

Its ability to capture the nostalgia of the Game Boy Advance era while boasting the refined design and execution of modern products demonstrates its creators' immeasurable talent, who also imbued it with an impressive thematic personality.

The critiques of various strategic production industries resonate with our current times, while in the process, the game leverages the yoyo as a central gameplay element to make traversal, combat, exploration, and customization an absolute delight that's also pretty unique.

Pipistrello and the Cursed Yoyo is among the finest indie titles I've enjoyed in the last couple of years, and anything other than recognizing it as brilliant is to underestimate its potential.

If you're interested in unfairly criticized titles, I recommend checking out these ten underrated action-adventure games that were badly reviewed.

As someone who didn't experience the franchise's heyday, Shinobi: Art of Vengeance not only caught me completely off guard, but I can think of very few resurrections that have felt so powerful.

The absolute masterwork of action and platforming that this title is results in frankly mind-blowing sequences, flowing seamlessly between both dimensions to deliver a thrilling adventure that doesn't let you breathe for a second.

As you unlock combos, powers, and movement abilities, you encounter an ever-increasing series of challenges that push the game's mechanics to their limits, which are meticulously refined to ensure you have complete control over every pixel.

Few titles in 2025 felt as satisfying to complete 100%, so Shinobi: Art of Vengeance stands as a masterfully unexpected example that, when you don't know what to do with your old IPs, outsourcing to an indie studio to work on them is the best possible idea.

The Legend of Zelda's heirs number in the hundreds of thousands, but that didn't stop Death's Door from emerging as one of its most intelligent and charismatic great-great-grandchildren.

The game perfectly understands the enjoyment that lies in discovery and exploration through iteration, inviting us on a diverse adventure with plenty of colorful biomes, enemies, characters, and circumstances that evoke all kinds of emotions.

Curiosity, fear, happiness, sadness, worry... The title silently manages a multitude of emotions both thematically and in its gameplay, limiting progression to allow for exploration of its expansive world at a prodigious pace.

When discussing isometric titles that combine action, platforming, and puzzles in the best possible way, Death's Door is at the description's forefront, though it's the ease and finesse with which it achieves this that's truly unexpected.

Castlevania is a series that went through some difficult years after the turn of the century, and it wasn't until Castlevania: Lords of Shadow arrived that the IP unexpectedly found some relief amidst so many setbacks, astonishingly becoming the best-selling game in the entire IP.

MercurySteam crafted one of the most beautiful and satisfying hack-and-slash games in the genre's history, finally adapting the saga's formula perfectly to 3D without losing an ounce of its core identity.

The whip feels better than ever, as do the movement mechanics, the puzzles, and the progression systems, all enhanced in the best possible way thanks to a magnificently crafted story and characters that keep you on the edge of your seat until the very last second.

Castlevania: Lords of Shadow completely forgoes being conceptually innovative, but the sacrifice, given it guaranteed us an extremely excellent title in all its facets, was totally worth it even when nobody was betting on such a resounding success.

If you enjoy discovering incredible titles without proper recognition, I recommend these ten best action-adventure games forgotten by time.

In the years when God of War slowed down, and Devil May Cry practically disappeared, a third icon emerged from absolutely nowhere to keep the hack-and-slash genre alive (with Bayonetta's permission): Darksiders.

Drawing inspiration from both Santa Monica Studio's work and The Legend of Zelda, Vigil Games crafted a splendidly elaborate tale and setting within Judeo-Christian mythology, one of the least explored in the video game industry.

Giant bosses based on satisfying gimmicks, varied and refreshing puzzles that make the most of War's arsenal and movement capabilities, phenomenal art design for both environments and enemies... It's the complete package.

That the company's first title would be such a presentation of knowledge in the genre's fundamentals was unforeseen even for the team itself, but much more for the hundreds of thousands who, like me, stumbled upon Darksiders by chance and have been hooked ever since.

Returning to the topic of franchises forced to reinvent themselves to adapt to modern times, not even the most optimistic projections for the Tomb Raider reboot could have predicted the absolute brilliance it would become.

Going back to Lara Croft's origins resulted in one of the quintessential action-adventure games of the entire generation, achieving an astonishing symphony of survival mechanics, realistic yet compelling gunplay, and simply fantastic settings and stories.

Each set piece surpasses the last, and the spectacular scenery contrasts perfectly with the brutal and tense story the legendary archaeologist faces, all while possessing the intriguing premise of not yet being the intrepid adventurer we all know, but rather a young, still-innocent graduate.

From concept to execution, Tomb Raider is a tapestry of sound decisions that, among other things, reminded Uncharted fans that Nathan Drake only managed to run because Lara Croft walked first.

I know that nowadays we're used to indie masterpieces being released every couple of months, but back in the day, before that was the norm, Hyper Light Drifter arrived like a hurricane of grandeur.

Taking Dark Souls' harshness, non-linearity, and disorientation, Heart Machine assembled an adventure that is both cryptic and captivating, where the gameplay and narrative direction are entirely in the player's hands.

Where to go and why are questions that only the user can answer through exploration, embarking on an intense and introspective journey where bosses and secrets abound, motivating us to explore every last corner of its silent world.

Along with gorgeous pixel art, a remarkable sound design, and a simple yet perfect gameplay loop, Hyper Light Drifter built a castle with just a few blocks, a feat reserved only for the greatest.

If you're looking for modern games to get lost in, I recommend the ten best adventure games of the 2020s (so far).

The reviews for Asura's Wrath were so scathing that it took me a decade to finally deign to play it, and the sheer shock of finishing it in tears when the credits rolled was beyond words.

Yes, every single one of the comments regarding its poor gameplay was, is, and will be correct, since it's a beat 'em up where the best parts of the gameplay are the on-rails shooting and quick-time events, and that doesn't exactly reflect well on a title of this genre.

Regardless, when you have such an incredible plot, abundant with charismatic characters, memorable scenes, robust mythology, and eccentrically immeasurable cinematics, I couldn't care less that every encounter boils down to mashing the attack button a hundred times a second.

The intercosmic scale that Asura's Wrath handles in its presentation and spectacle is monumental, unique, and emotional enough to overlook its mediocre mechanics, being the only case in the entire genre where it's a masterpiece even without being fun in the traditional sense.

Bucking the trend in the genre, whose disdain for stealth became evident in the last decade, Arkane Studios made a tremendous statement in 2012 with Dishonored's release.

Coming from another underrated gem, Dark Messiah of Might and Magic, the studio embraced the lingering force of immersive sims and materialized it into a project that excels as both a stealth game and an action-adventure game.

Boasting some of the best level design in video game history, along with a story that evolves based on the player's actions rather than dialogue choices, Dishonored is one of the century's defining power fantasies.

Between Dunwall's perfect setting, the outstanding array of gadgets and powers that allow for creative and expansive use of stealth and violence, and the majestic musical pieces that accompany us, it's clear that the love for stealth games will never die as long as this IP remains available.

Years go by, and I still wonder if the genre has enough strength to produce a title that surpasses Dishonored, but for now, it remains on the throne without any concern about being replaced.

In this list, we'll discuss our picks for the best action-adventure games that go beyond a hundred hours, from RPGs to known open-world titles.

Google says ad tags in server-side Tag Manager will accept full IP addresses while Analytics tags stay redacted. Here's what changes and how to redact.

Server-side Google Tag Manager will let tags for Google’s ad products accept full IP addresses starting Jan. 11, 2027, according to a Google notice to advertisers.

The change covers tags that send data to Google Ads, Display & Video 360, Search Ads 360 and Campaign Manager 360 from a server container. Google Analytics tags will keep redacting IP addresses, the notice says.

Google Ads specialist Adriaan Dekker shared the notice on LinkedIn on Nov. 2. It carries no issue date, and Google’s Tag Manager release notes don’t mention the change as of Nov. 3.

Advertisers with Google ad tags in a server container have until Jan. 11 to decide whether those tags should get full or partially redacted IP addresses.

The notice, titled “Updates to network signal processing in server-side tagging,” explains that server containers “will transition from container-wide IP address redaction to individual tag-level processing.” It then outlines four effects:

Tags sending data to Google Ads, Display & Video 360, Search Ads 360 and Campaign Manager 360 will now accept the full IP address.

Google Analytics tags will continue to apply IP redaction.

Third-party tags will maintain independent control over the signals that they accept.

Transformations can redact these signals before any tag accesses the data.

The notice addresses server containers only. It says nothing about the Google tag or web containers running in the browser.

Google’s release notes show server-side Tag Manager could redact or anonymize IPs through Analytics server tags as of March 2021. Transformations, which edit or exclude event data before tags read it, followed in June 2023.

The notice doesn’t explain how container-wide redaction is currently set up. Google’s server-side setup page for the Google Ads conversion tag says the tag sends “all the available conversion data to Google” unless transformations change it, and, as of Nov. 3, doesn’t mention IP addresses.

The notice directs users to “our Help Centre article for detailed opt-out instructions” and does not reveal where the link is pointing to based upon the screenshot provided. A Tag Manager Help Center page, IP address redaction for server-side tagging, shows an example of how to use transformations.

The page outlines the main steps to set up this functionality using transformations:

This will affect the tags you choose (such as Google Ads or Floodlight). After completing these steps the page also states to check the results in Preview prior to deploying the changes to production. Additionally, the page explains that Analytics server tags “already apply partial IP redaction by default.”

Google’s API reference says the function zeroes the last octet of an IPv4 address and the last 80 bits of an IPv6 address. The Help page calls the result a “partially redacted IP.”

Google added the function to its API reference after Oct. 12. The Internet Archive’s Oct. 12 capture of the page doesn’t list it. The live page does, and shows an Oct. 30 update date.

Some third-party tags may need a Transformation of their own. The Help page notes that these tags can read IP addresses from custom headers or event parameters rather than from ip_override, so you’ll need to set the Transformation to target the field each tag uses.

Per the notice, advertisers utilizing Google’s advertising products “must ensure that proper end-user notices and legally required consents are in place before data collection begins.” In addition, all advertisers using Google’s advertising products in the EEA “must strictly adhere” to Google’s EU User Consent Policy, and the policy extends to those customers whose end users reside in the United Kingdom and/or Switzerland. All customers in other areas where privacy regulations apply must follow those regional requirements and platform terms.

Google says the update “integrates privacy-enhancing technologies (PETs),” filters spam and invalid traffic, and will improve campaign performance.

The announcement does not provide information about how changes will interact with consent mode as well as the non-consenting requests that were stated in the Release Notes (Floodlight tags in server containers are said to have begun sending in July 2025) nor was there any mention of where or how long full IP addresses would be stored.

The announced server-side change is distinct from Google’s planned Analytics data control updates; these will encrypt IP addresses collected by the Google tag and SDK and pass them to linked Google Ads accounts, on dates Google plans to share later this year.

You can use server-side tagging to decide how much information goes with each tag as it leaves your servers, and Google’s notice determines whether its ad tags will accept full IP addresses in this configuration.

Prior to Jan. 11, review all Google Ads, Floodlight and third-party tags inside your server containers and identify which IP field each tag is reading. The method outlined by Google in the help section on how to build a Transformation using preview testing is currently the documented solution for keeping an ad tag’s IP partially masked. Your consent process should include any IP data used by these tags.

Tag-level IP processing for Google server containers is scheduled to begin January 11, 2027. There are no references to IP addresses in either of Google’s server-side pages on Transformations or consent mode as of Nov. 3.

The Tag Manager release notes may provide an official announcement when tag-level IP handling for server containers is available and the developer pages may have the detailed information. The Redaction help page should be reviewed prior to creating your Transformation because that is how you do this now.

7 Mistakes Founders and CEOs Make When Talking to the Media (and How to Avoid Them)

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Even accomplished business leaders make mistakes during media interviews. Here’s how to avoid seven common missteps that can undermine your credibility and cost you valuable coverage.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Being a successful CEO doesn’t automatically make you a great media spokesperson. I’ve coached executives across financial services and other professional industries who can lead organizations, navigate complex challenges and command a boardroom, yet struggle to communicate effectively with journalists.

Even the most accomplished executives can undermine their credibility by talking too much, making unsupported claims or turning interviews into sales pitches. Fortunately, these mistakes are avoidable. Here are seven I see most often and how to prevent them.

One of the biggest misconceptions executives have is that they must answer every question a journalist asks. You don’t. Even when a question relates to your industry, it may fall outside your specific expertise.

Rather than guessing, say, “I’m not the best person to answer that, but I can connect you with someone who is,” or “That’s outside my area of expertise, but what I can tell you is…” Journalists don’t expect you to know everything. They do expect you to know what you’re talking about. Of course, declining to speculate isn’t the same as evading a legitimate, difficult question about your business.

A journalist asks a straightforward question, and the executive launches into a five-minute explanation filled with background, caveats and unnecessary details. By the time they make their point, the reporter may have lost interest or run out of time.

My advice? Lead with the headline. Answer the question directly in one or two sentences, then stop. If the journalist wants more, offer an anecdote or compelling example that illustrates your point. Journalists love stories, but don’t bury your main message beneath layers of explanation. If the reporter remembers only one sentence, make sure it’s the one that matters.

Before every media interview, I coach clients to identify three key messages they want to communicate. Not 10. Not 15. Three. These might relate to your business, expertise, product or the issue being discussed.

Ask yourself: If only three things I say make it into the story, what should they be? Even when a journalist calls unexpectedly on deadline, it’s reasonable to ask, “Can I call you back in five minutes?” Use that time to organize your thoughts, assuming the reporter’s deadline allows it.

Don’t be afraid to reinforce important messages naturally throughout the conversation. Preparation isn’t about memorizing a script; it’s about knowing what matters before the interview begins.

Financial services, healthcare, biotechnology and other specialized industries are filled with acronyms, credentials and technical terminology. But just because a term is familiar to you doesn’t mean the journalist understands it.

I encourage executives to explain complicated concepts as though they’re talking to a family member around the dinner table. Could your mother, grandmother or 10-year-old understand your point? If not, simplify it.

And don’t confuse a journalist’s unfamiliarity with industry terminology for a lack of intelligence. Reporters cover multiple subjects, often under tight deadlines. A question that seems obvious to you may be exactly what their readers need answered. You’re the subject-matter expert. Help them understand.

A media interview isn’t a sales presentation. Journalists aren’t interviewing you to hear how wonderful your company is. They’re looking for information, perspective and insights that help them tell a compelling story.

Statements like “We’re the best in the industry” or “Our firm offers unparalleled service” aren’t particularly useful. Excessive self-promotion can immediately undermine your credibility.

Instead, share a surprising insight, explain an emerging trend, offer a contrarian perspective or provide a real-world example. It’s fine to provide background about your firm, but don’t hijack the conversation with a commercial.

The best media sources make the journalist’s story better, not their own company sound bigger.

Unsupported statistics, exaggerated claims and overconfident predictions can quickly damage your credibility, especially when discussing financial markets, economic conditions or other complex subjects where forecasts are inherently uncertain.

If you cite a statistic, know where it came from and be prepared to provide the source. If you’re making a prediction, distinguish your opinion from established fact. And if you don’t know an answer, say so. There’s nothing wrong with telling a reporter, “I don’t have that figure in front of me, but I can check and follow up.”

Journalists remember sources they can trust. Don’t give them a reason to question yours.

When speaking with a journalist, assume you’re on the record. That includes casual remarks, offhand observations and comments made before or after the formal interview.

I strongly caution executives about going “off the record.” Many misunderstand what that means or assume they can declare something off the record after saying it. They can’t. Such arrangements require mutual agreement before information is shared, and I generally advise clients to avoid them unless there’s a compelling reason and clearly established terms.

The safest approach is simple: Don’t say anything to a journalist that you wouldn’t be comfortable seeing in print or hearing repeated publicly.

A successful media interview isn’t about demonstrating how intelligent you are, answering every question perfectly or finding opportunities to promote your business. It’s about being a credible, knowledgeable and useful source.

The best spokespeople prepare without sounding rehearsed, offer insights rather than commercials and know when to stop talking. Ultimately, being quotable isn’t about saying more. It’s about having something meaningful to say and knowing how to say it clearly.

Being a successful CEO doesn’t automatically make you a great media spokesperson. I’ve coached executives across financial services and other professional industries who can lead organizations, navigate complex challenges and command a boardroom, yet struggle to communicate effectively with journalists.

Even the most accomplished executives can undermine their credibility by talking too much, making unsupported claims or turning interviews into sales pitches. Fortunately, these mistakes are avoidable. Here are seven I see most often and how to prevent them.

One of the biggest misconceptions executives have is that they must answer every question a journalist asks. You don’t. Even when a question relates to your industry, it may fall outside your specific expertise.

Ford Settles Transit Trail Class Action Lawsuit By Paying Owners Less Than The Package Cost

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The current trend of automakers "off-roadifying" their road cars has created a new breed of hiked up, black plastic-clad soft roaders that buyers can't seem to get enough of. Ford gave its Transit full-size van the treatment back in 2022 with the all-wheel-drive Transit Trail, but recalled all of them in early 2024 and replaced their 30.5-inch all-terrain tires that rubbed against the wheel wells with the same 28.5-inch tires on standard Transits, and owners were not pleased.

They were so displeased, in fact, that they filed a class action lawsuit that we first reported on last year. Ford finally reached a settlement with the plaintiffs last week, offering Transit Trail owners a $6,000 payout for the diminished off-road abilities of their vans. This is a pretty healthy payout from a class action lawsuit settlement, but it's still less than half of the original price of the Trail package that cost $12,500 when new.

According to classaction.org, "Eligible for the settlement are all individuals who, as the original owner or lessee, bought or leased a model year 2023 or 2024 Ford Transit Trail before June 1, 2024 for personal, family or household use and who have not already received any compensation from Ford, including any settlement payment or repurchase, related to a covered vehicle or to any alleged defect of, or economic loss relating to, the vehicle." More fine print about how to prove membership in the settlement class is available on the Class Action site.

The Ford Transit Trail was marketed by Ford as having an "adventure-ready chassis is re-tuned and tested to meet the same BUILT FORD TOUGH durability standards as the rest of the Transit lineup," complete with 6.7-inches of ground clearance, upgraded underbody protection, and the 30.5-inch Goodyear Wrangler Workhorse tires that led to the recall. The 28.5-inch tires that Ford replaced the Wranglers with reduced the ground clearance of Transit Trails down to 5.5-inches, or the same as the standard Transit. Nearly 2,000 vehicles were included in the recall, but it's not immediately clear how many owners are eligible for this class action lawsuit settlement.

Participation in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico VPP program doubled in 2026

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Sunrun announced that participation in its Puerto Rico distributed power plant has doubled to more than 60,000 customers, representing nearly 70,000 home batteries. The distributed power plant frequently and reliably delivers 45 MW of dispatchable power for four hours at a time, providing critical support to Puerto Rico’s electric grid.

Since April, Sunrun has dispatched its power plant more than 60 times to weather severe energy shortfalls and prevent rolling blackouts. During an extended period of unseasonably hot temperatures this fall, Sunrun dispatched its home batteries to the grid for 18 consecutive days beginning October 13.

Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant has scaled rapidly since 2024, when approximately 4,000 customers were enrolled. Enrollment surged to more than 30,000 customers in 2025. Sunrun is the largest aggregator in Puerto Rico’s Customer Battery Energy Sharing program.

Puerto Rico’s electric utility operator, LUMA, said in June and again in July that energy from home batteries helped “maintain system stability and prevent outages that would have affected thousands of customers.”

“What began in Puerto Rico out of necessity for family resilience has become a vital blueprint for the U.S. mainland, where severe weather and the proliferation of AI data centers are demanding new energy solutions,” said Sunrun CEO Mary Powell. “Sunrun is uniquely positioned to provide these solutions, whether to support a grid at its breaking point or to provide capacity for hyperscalers looking to come online.”

Sunrun’s increased dispatching capacity in Puerto Rico comes after residents on the island experienced an average of 36 hours of power outages in 2025 that were not caused by major adverse weather, which is 19% more than the previous year, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration. When severe weather is factored in, residents experienced an additional 23 hours of power interruptions.

Customers who participate in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant choose how much energy to share, with most contributing a small portion of their total battery capacity. This approach ensures they retain reserve power for personal backup power in the event of grid outages. Participants are financially compensated for the power they choose to share, while Sunrun generates recurring revenue by operating the distributed power plant.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

The Busy Mom’s Guide To Gift Shopping On Budget

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Amazon and Alexa for Shopping make it easy to get it all for less.

Amazon knows how hard moms are working, and they have some ways to lighten the load. When you shop through Alexa for Shopping — Amazon’s personalized agentic shopping assistant — it all becomes way less stressful and way more affordable, and you might even get enough time back to finish your coffee before it gets cold (can you imagine?).

With everyday low prices that meet or beat other retailers across a huge selection of products, from affordable private-brands that help you save without sacrificing quality to the name-brand finds your kids have been asking for, Amazon makes it easier to find gifts that fit your budget.

Here are a few ways to use Amazon to get all the gifts you need — and stay sane while doing it.

Alexa for Shopping is Amazon’s agentic shopping assistant, and it’s really something else. It combines knowledge of the hundreds of millions of products across Amazon with info from across the web, and applies all of that to your personal shopping history and preferences. In other words, it knows all the possible gifts you could get on Amazon, and it knows you.

You can use Alexa for Shopping by asking it questions, like “What are the best toys for 5 year-olds?” or “What are teenagers asking for this year?” or even “What are some premium and more affordable brand options for a red dress and matching headband for my 3 year-old daughter’s holiday outfit?” It will give you ideas (including taking your shopping history and preferences into account) and then share a list of products to look through. So whether you’re gifting your own kids and just need some fresh ideas when you’re drawing a blank, or shopping for an older niece who lives five states away and have no clue where to start, you get tailored advice that helps you land on the perfect thing.

Budgeting during the holidays is not for the weak. Between finding the perfect gifts for your kids, your mother-in-law, and your spouse, it’s easy to get caught up in the excitement of holiday shopping and discover more things you know they’ll (or you’ll) love along the way.

The good news? Amazon’s prices consistently meet or beat other retailers, so you’re already getting low, competitive prices when you shop on Amazon. On top of that, Alexa for Shopping is a great tool to help you stay on track. First of all, you can use it so see up to a full year of price history for any product you’re interested in. Once you have that information, you can ask Alexa for Shopping to let you know when it drops to the right price for you. And even better, you can ask Alexa for Shopping to turn on auto-buy so it can complete your purchase using your default payment method when the item reaches your price target.

That saves you time (who has the bandwidth to keep checking back every day?!) and helps you know that when you do hit “buy” (or have Alexa do it for you), you’re getting the thing you want at a great price. And if you do that for all of your holiday shopping, you’re setting yourself up to spend less this season. That can be a huge relief at such an expensive time of year.

Bonus: the savings aren't just about gifts. From stocking up on groceries for the holiday feast to getting extra household essentials for overnight guests, the season comes with plenty of demands. With everyday low prices on fresh groceries and household essentials, plus fast free delivery, you can get the things you need at great prices that help your holiday budget go further.

A large part of the mental mom load is figuring out which [insert kid-related item here] is the best one. From onesies to bikes to pimple patches, there are so many things to choose from, and it takes time to figure out what’s actually right for your kid.

Alexa for Shopping takes that stress off your plate, too. Once you’ve found a few options that might work, Alexa for Shopping’s got you: it can show you side-by-side comparisons, highlighting the differences in features, price points, and even the reviews. Instead of digging through details, you get it all laid out and can quickly make your pick. Goodbye, overthinking.

We all know that holiday magic is really just... moms. But Amazon and the features built into Alexa for Shopping sort of feel like their own kind of magic. With Amazon, you can create a holiday season your kids will always remember, and make it look almost effortless. When you can do that without overspending or screaming into the void? We think that’s beautiful.

(And heads up, the savings go even further with Prime thanks to free delivery, exclusive deals, gas discounts, and more. But we digress.)

The best kind of holiday magic isn't just finding the right gifts at the right prices. It's getting a little time back for yourself, too. And honestly, moms deserve that kind of magic most of all.

Amazon and Prime deliver billions in savings every year — averaging over $1,000 per customer for 100+ million customers. Learn more about how Amazon makes the holidays and everyday life more affordable for families here.

New York Studio School revamp unearths long-forgotten murals

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Back in 1907, Gertrude Vanderbilt Whitney established her sculpture studio in a converted stable and hayloft on MacDougal Alley in the heart of Manhattan’s Greenwich Village. The heiress and artist then proceeded to procure four neighbouring brownstones one by one, with the Lincoln Memorial sculptor Daniel Chester French being the last holdout.

The five buildings that now comprise 8 West 8th Street are some of the New York art world’s most storied. It was here that Whitney promoted US artists at a time when they were widely denigrated, and where she opened the Whitney Museum of American Art in 1931. After the museum relocated uptown, the nascent New York Studio School (NYSS) moved into the compound in 1967.

Over the past century Edward Hopper sketched, John Cage lectured and Philip Guston taught within these walls. Countless other greats practised, exhibited or, in the case of Marianne Faithfull, lived in what is now a National Historic Landmark. “This building has been the incubator for American art for 120 years,” says the artist Matthew Ritchie, sitting in a bare-bones conference room on a warm September afternoon. It has also been sorely in need of a makeover at least since he was a student here in the 1990s.

When Ritchie was named the school’s director last year, the roof and studio skylights—coveted by students for their diffused natural light—leaked, the nearly century-old cloth-wrapped wiring had deteriorated dangerously and wheelchair access was extremely limited. His priority was renovation. The first phase—including doubling the public exhibition space and creating a new accessible lecture and performance hall in wood with stepped seating—is set to be completed this autumn, and phase two will begin in the spring.

Prior to Ritchie’s arrival, the school had obtained public grants for the necessary work, but they required matching funds and their expiration date was fast approaching. He quickly marshalled forces. The board agreed to donate the required money, he says, and the school tapped Matthew Baird of Baird Architects to helm the overhaul.

The renovation work uncovered an early work by the British-born artist Cecily Brown painted on the wall when she was a student there in the 1990s

Ritchie and Baird’s history of collaboration dates back 17 years and across two other local institutions. In 2009, they teamed up to consider the effects of rising sea levels on the built environment as part of the interdisciplinary Rising Currents project at the Museum of Modern Art. Just a few years later, they put their theories into practice after Hurricane Sandy tore through the city, its storm surge flooding much of downtown. Ritchie was on the board of the beloved alternative art space The Kitchen when it enlisted Baird to rehabilitate its waterlogged lobby and black-box theatre.

The architect’s sensitive execution on that project—he preserved visual evidence of flood damage, for example—made him an obvious choice for the NYSS project, which presented a similar challenge. “You have this really cool building that’s got this patina of great artists that have moved through it,” Baird says. “How do you not erase that?”

Thanks to Baird and Ritchie’s handle-with-care edict, the construction crew was on the lookout for treasures buried beneath layers of plasterboard. The artist Cecily Brown had told Ritchie that she painted her first mural on a school wall when she was a student in the 1990s but could not remember where. And the school archives held numerous photos of murals Whitney had commissioned from the artist Robert E. Locher for the original carriage house.

Sure enough, the crew uncovered the work of both artists within a 24-hour period. Brown’s piece—featuring disembodied penises, two of them appearing to kiss—is in the newly expanded gallery space on the main floor. To prevent it from potentially distracting from exhibitions, the design team installed a shallow cabinet in front of the mural.

Ritchie jokes that the set-up resembles a gabinetto segreto, or secret room, the most famous of which was a locked vault of erotic artefacts from Pompeii and Herculaneum. “Who doesn’t want a Cecily Brown mural? And then, who doesn’t want pornography in your brand-new public gallery?” he says with a laugh. “It’s the perfect combination.”

A recently uncovered mural by the artist Robert E. Locher, commissioned by Gertrude Vanderbilt Whitney

Workers discovered the two Locher murals where a bathroom and a hallway leading to it will be built. Intended to be viewed in the candlelight of Whitney’s dining room, one is a glimmering green, the other a trompe l’oeil image of doric columns. Baird tweaked the layout but not the location of the restroom. “Now it will be the most historic bathroom in New York,” Ritchie says.

A less fortuitous surprise: the beams beneath Robert Winthrop Chanler’s flame-motif fireplace in Whitney’s original second-floor studio were cracking, requiring emergency shoring up to stop the ceiling from collapse—together with the priceless fireplace. “This is the urgency of renovation,” Ritchie says. “You can wait too long, and then you lose it all.”

Because of the grant deadlines for the first phase of the project, Baird needed to work at warp speed, condensing three years of planning, permitting, bidding and execution into one. Owing to New York City’s complex building code, he approached the $2.2m renovation as “100 small projects” rather than gutting the 59,000 sq. ft building. The landmark-restoration specialist Jonathan Raible is overseeing the new roof.

In addition to enhancing the first floor’s public spaces, Baird rewired the building, renovated the wreck of a kitchen and improved accessibility by adding a wheelchair lift to the performance hall. “You can’t have a public programme if you don’t provide correct facilities for the public,” Ritchie says. “You’re not really welcoming them.”

Climbing the narrow staircases to the maze of studios where the school’s 27 full-time, in-person students work, Baird speaks enthusiastically about the second phase of renovations, which will include a student coffee bar. In keeping with his preservationist instincts, he plans to relamp with LED bulbs the elegant Fresnel recessed box lighting that Whitney installed for evenly washed walls, and he hopes to restore the system of baffles that modulated direct light in the studios as well as undertake other restoration while safeguarding the landmark’s character.

Ritchie already has his eye on another, likely longer-term fix for the school: free tuition. “That would require a very large endowment from extremely generous single or multiple donors,” he says, giving his best impression of a silver-tongued salesman, “which we’re eagerly awaiting with an entirely unique naming opportunity.”

During Weinberg’s tenure, the Whitney built and moved into its new Meatpacking District home, saw attendance increase threefold and navigated a series of scandals

The 82nd edition of the most closely-watched recurring exhibition in the United States will open in spring 2026

The institution's revamped third and fourth floors present reconfigured galleries, expanded education spaces and a luminous display of more than 100 menorahs from around the world

The building, just a few blocks from the museum, will be renovated and used as a permanent home for the Whitney’s Independent Study Program

Dodge Rejoins NASCAR Cup Series After 14-Year Absence

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The entire Big Three will be battling for stock car supremacy in 2027. Dodge announced Monday that it will return to the NASCAR Cup Series next season. Kaulig Racing's pair of Dodge Chargers will be the first to compete in stock car racing's marquee series since the manufacturer's departure after the 2012 season. Plenty of questions remain after the announcement, including who will drive the entries and what the Gen 7 Charger's bodywork will look like.

The team partnering with Dodge was never a question. Kaulig Racing first paired up with Ram for the brand's return to the NASCAR Craftsman Truck Series this season, fielding five Ram trucks. It's surprising how quickly Stellantis North America cobbled together a Cup program. NASCAR confirmed that Dodge completed its wind tunnel testing and will have its production body panels ready later this month. Tim Kuniskis, Stellantis' Head of American Brands, said:

"Everyone said it was impossible to get back in one year. We said, 'watch this,' Sure, we could spend three years planning, testing and perfecting ... or we could do all three in real time, on the track. It's going to be exponentially harder this way. But slow has never been part of Dodge DNA. LFG."

The biggest reason why a Dodge program for the Cup Series was organized in only a year is that the manufacturer will not be building its own engines. Dodge has yet to announce where it will source Kaulig's 2027 motors, but it is in talks with a third party. The Athletic reported that ECR Engines could potentially build engines for Dodge. The partnership has issues because ECR currently produces Chevrolet engines and is owned by Richard Childress, a Chevrolet team owner. NASCAR, Ford and Toyota would need to approve an exemption for ECR to build engines for both Chevy and Dodge.

Besides the powerplant, Kaulig expects to announce its 2027 Cup Series driver lineup this weekend ahead of the season finale at Homestead-Miami Speedway. It wouldn't be a shock if the team retained its current pairing of Ty Dillon and A.J. Allmendinger. However, the driver market has seen a major shakeup over the past month. Brad Keselowski is departing RFK Racing, a team he co-owns, after falling out with fellow co-owners Fenway Sports Group. He stated last weekend that he will be available to race elsewhere regardless of manufacturer. Keselowski won Dodge's most recent Cup Series title with Penske in 2012. The 42-year-old admitted that he hasn't discussed anything with Dodge or Kaulig, but he's open to the idea.

JetBlue Leads The Industry In Raising Checked Bag Fees… Again!

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

For whatever reason, JetBlue tends to be the industry leader when it comes to raising checked bag fees, and competitors tend to follow. Earlier this year, JetBlue was the first major US carrier to raise checked bag fees, and now the airline is raising checked bag fees again…

Effective immediately (for tickets purchased as of November 3, 2026), JetBlue has increased checked bag fees. JetBlue has a rather complicated fee structure for checked bags, with both off-peak and peak pricing, plus different pricing based on whether you pay for the bag 24 hours or more in advance.

Below is a chart with JetBlue’s new checked bag fees.

As you can see, an off-peak first checked bag on a domestic flight will now cost you $50-60 (depending on whether you pay in advance), while a peak first checked bag on a domestic flight will now cost you $55-70 (depending on whether you pay in advance). For context, below are the dates that are considered peak for the purposes of bag fees.

It goes without saying that paying $50-70 for a first checked bag is a pretty substantial cost, especially when you consider how little checked bags used to cost. On a one-way, you’re looking at $100-140. For a family of four, you’d be looking at $400-560.

When JetBlue raised checked bag fees earlier this year, the airline released a statement claiming it was increasing checked bag fees to be “more competitive,” and that “while we recognize that fee increases are never ideal, we take careful consideration to ensure these changes are implemented only when necessary.” I imagine this time around, the logic is much of the same.

The simple reality is that oil prices are way up. Airlines have actually done a solid job of being able to make fare increases stick. But that’s not enough to fully offset the increase in the cost of jet fuel.

JetBlue is in a rough financial spot, and it hasn’t turned a profit since before the pandemic. The airline is hoping to turn an operating profit in 2027, though that strikes me as being particularly optimistic. So the logic for raising checked bags is threefold:

JetBlue has just increased checked bag fees once again, and you’ll now pay $50-70 for a first checked bag on a domestic flight. This is JetBlue’s second price increase on checked bags so far this year. When airlines are trying to increase revenue, checked bag fees are often low hanging fruit.

When competitors match, it also becomes something that’s unavoidable for consumers, other than getting a credit card… which also has upside for the airline! I’d be shocked if other airlines don’t match this move from JetBlue.

Severe Flooding Impacts CDT Trail Towns in Colorado and New Mexico

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Severe rainfall across southwestern Colorado has triggered major flooding, widespread infrastructure damage, and mandatory evacuations in the CDT trail towns of Pagosa Springs, Colorado (NOBO mile 866.4) and Chama, New Mexico (NOBO mile 703.5).

In Pagosa Springs, the San Juan River reached major flood thresholds this weekend for the second time in about two weeks. Local authorities issued urgent evacuation orders on Saturday for low-lying zones along local rivers, fearing a potentially “catastrophic” flood event that authorities warned could be even worse than the historic flood of 1911, which wiped out most of the town’s infrastructure at the time.

The river ultimately crested at 16.81 feet on Sunday — significantly lower than feared, but still one of the most significant floods in Pagosa Springs history. Water levels rose enough to completely inundate municipal areas and damage or destroy roads and bridges. Portions of the highway through downtown are closed due to washouts.

The river runs directly through the center of town in Pagosa Springs. This photo was taken in June 2011 and shows the river at its normal volume. Photo via David

The floodwaters began to recede on Monday but left widespread infrastructure damage in their wake. Evacuation orders remain in place. Emergency response agencies and state infrastructure crews are under extreme strain as communities grapple with the aftereffects of these two disastrous, back-to-back flood events.

Flooding has been widespread throughout the southwest this fall as the region has received heavy rainfall driven by a strong El Niño year, with large swaths of southern Utah and cities including Sedona, Arizona and Durango, Colorado also badly affected.

A post shared by Since JulEYE (@sincejuleye)

While this area of the CDT sees fewer hikers this late in the year, the intense flooding has still impacted trail access. In Chama, New Mexico, officials have ordered a mandatory evacuation for residents and businesses from Elkhorn Restaurant south to Unser’s at Highway 64/84.

In a statement, the Continental Divide Trail Coalition stated that “The extent of damage to the CDT, access roads, and CDT communities is still unknown … Trail towns and communities are at the heart of the CDT experience. Our thoughts are with our friends and the community members on the ground in the areas affected by these extreme weather events, and we hope everyone stays safe.”

Featured image: The San Juan River running through Pagosa Springs in June 2011 (not in a flood stage in this photo). Photo via David

Dia Art Foundation Teams Up with MCA Australia on Two-Part Exhibition for Indigenous Artist d harding

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Executive Editor, ARTnews & Art in America

New York’s Dia Art Foundation will co-present a two-part exhibition of work by the Indigenous Australian artist d harding to be split between Dia Chelsea and the Museum of Contemporary Art Australia in Sydney. The New York component will open in February, with the other part slated for March; both will run into August of next year.

“A through line in harding’s practice is the concept of transfer—a consideration of how histories are carried through materials, techniques, and places, transporting ancestral knowledge into dialogue with the spaces in which their work is encountered,” Dia curator Jordan Carter said in a statement. “Presented in parallel at two institutions, bridging Australia and the U.S., this two-part exhibition reveals the iterative logic, conceptual depth, and cultural resonance of harding’s practice.” Related Articles Jessica Morgan Named Top Director at Tate, Departing Dia After More Than a Decade Museum Veteran Philippe Vergne Joins Miami's Bass Museum as Artistic Director and Chief Curator

For the Dia show, harding has drawn inspiration from a number of site visits to the foundation’s storied Land art holdings in the American West—which include Walter De Maria’s The Lightning Field, Robert Smithson’s Spiral Jetty, and Nancy Holt’s Sun Tunnels—as well as rock-art galleries on harding’s ancestral Country in Central Queensland. A new commission for the Dia exhibition is a series of “transfer paintings” involving canvases that harding inherited from fellow artist Gordon Bennett, processed with earthen materials, and then transferred to 60 panels that evoke locations with a significant sense of place.

The show at MCA Australia, harding’s first museum survey, will be organized around four themes—inheritance, visitation, embodiment, and continuity—and will include an expansion of the artist’s “wall composition,” titled Know them in correct judgement (2026) and made in collaboration with extended family members in mind of their inheritance of rock-art.

Of harding’s importance at home and abroad, MCA Australia senior curator Anneke Jaspers said, “Their work powerfully recalibrates dominant notions of the sovereign, the center, and the canon from an Aboriginal perspective.”

AI Data Centers Have a PR Problem. OpenAI Is Proposing a Rebrand

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

As part of the company's post-midterms marketing campaign, data centers could be called “AI compute campuses.”

OpenAI is shifting its marketing strategy after the US midterm elections conclude this week, proposing to rebrand its data centers as “AI compute campuses.” The creator of ChatGPT is preparing a public relations campaign that could include regulatory lobbying and efforts to rehabilitate AI’s image, according to Axios.

The push coincides with a potential blue wave in Congress. OpenAI Global Affairs Officer Chris Lehane is reportedly trying to steer the AI policy debate and to send a message that “AI should be built by and for people,” as Axios reports.

The effort is a response to an increasingly enraged electorate, as backlash to AI data center construction becomes a unifying issue across party lines. A July Gallup poll found that as many as 70% of US adults are opposed to local data center construction, including Democrats, Republicans and independents.

Supporters of AI data centers often argue that the construction and operation of these massive AI server farms bring job growth to local communities. But studies show that employment is limited, and in some cases temporary. Residents continue to rally against the environmental impacts of water contamination, the burning of fossil fuels and noise pollution.

Lehane told Axios that OpenAI views the conversation as healthy and that “communities should hold companies responsible and accountable so they really and truly get a transformative benefit from these projects.” The company is trying to reframe AI adoption as a means to access economic opportunities.

OpenAI’s new PR campaign aligns with Silicon Valley’s effort to repackage the controversial technology so it can move full speed ahead with building more data centers, which the White House has touted as vital engines for the US economy. President Donald Trump’s recent move to rename AI as “super intelligence” was another bid to dress up the capabilities of frontier labs, which have been under increased financial scrutiny by investors.

A representative from OpenAI didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

OpenAI’s post-midterms marketing package includes implementing speed limits and mandatory safeguards for AI model development, largely echoing Anthropic’s “pacing” talking points. The company is also trying to shape the international standards that dictate the technology’s development.

Up to now, a lack of regulatory oversight has left top AI labs largely unchecked, leading to incidents where frontier models slipped past flimsy safety guardrails and caused real-world disruptions across the web.

OpenAI’s lobbying strategy for an alleged “human-centered” path based on “trust” fits squarely in the company’s data center rebrand — all calculated to help deflect public criticism. If the company publicly advocates slowing its own AI development, it can be more effective in what critics call “regulatory capture,” a tactic that gives large AI companies significant advantages.

In other words, if OpenAI helps write the rules meant to govern the industry, it can shape regulations to protect its market dominance. If frontier labs are considered the experts in constraining the industry, they can create a legal moat around themselves, making it more difficult for smaller competitors to enter the AI industry, while protecting their bottom lines from any real damage.

The Trump administration has been a major boon to the “Magnificent Seven,” the group of major tech companies that heavily influence the S&P 500 and global financial markets. After cozying up to the White House during the past two years, the most influential figures in Silicon Valley have been rewarded with a lax regulatory environment and expensive federal contracts.

Many of the biggest tech and AI companies donated millions of dollars to the president’s inaugural fund and, more recently, to his $330 million White House ballroom project. (Apple and Nvidia were among the top contributors, at $25 million each.)

As it stands, Trump is working directly with AI companies to define security threats and respond on their own terms. If the political tide turns in DC, however, it would make sense for Big Tech to make contingency plans for operating in a potentially less favorable environment. Democratic politicians have generally eyed the AI gold rush with greater outward skepticism and scrutiny, with some members of Congress even backing a resolution opposing AI company bailouts in the event of a financial bubble popping.

Lehane told Axios he believes AI will be the primary issue voters care about in the next presidential election cycle, and that the midterms are a “bit of a movie trailer for what you’re going to see in 2028.” If nothing else, OpenAI is angling to make new political alliances before that time comes.
