Space Mirrors Promise Daylight in the Dark, But Rescue Teams Aren’t Convinced

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A floodlight operated by Reflect Orbital illuminates the night sky. (Photo: Kern County Sheriff’s Office)

In the middle of a dark night on the foothills of Mt. Whitney, onlookers were suddenly flooded by a cone of light 3 miles wide by 10 miles long.

The source was a high-powered floodlight, placed nearby as part of a test exercise by a startup called Reflect Orbital. The Los Angeles-based company’s mission is to “launch the future of sunlight” by placing a swarm of mirrored satellites in low Earth orbit built to redirect sunlight anywhere on Earth on demand. The ground-based floodlight was designed to mimic that effect.

Among a wide range of use cases including agriculture, entertainment, and defense, the company claims rescue as one potential use of its tech in the coming years.

On a country road outside Lone Pine, California, a handful of seasoned rescue volunteers and public safety officials would find out how much — if any — a light field roughly the size of Manhattan and twice as bright as a full moon would improve their chances.

Phillip Cash, operations leader for China Lake Mountain Rescue Group, said his team arrived at the site with optimism.

“My team is largely scientists, engineers, technicians,” he told DarkSky International. “We live for this sort of stuff.”

Group leaders reported to the San Francisco Chronicle and DarkSky International that they envisioned very limited uses for the technology in rescue. They struggled with glare, saw little promise of benefit for helicopter crews, and realized the light could also shut off unexpectedly.

In the test, China Lake MRG and Inyo County SAR rehearsed standard search-and-rescue tasks twice — once in the dark, once under the big light. Crews ran grid searches, stretcher carries, and rope rigging.

Cash first noticed the light made it easier to move around on the ground. His peripheral vision was improved, he told DarkSky, and he didn’t have to worry about stepping in a hole.

But from there, the efficacy mostly faded.

Glare was an issue. While that may or may not be the case with a satellite as the source, Cash noted most rescuers already wear hats at night for this very reason. Limiting any light from the sky or ambient sources preserves night vision.

Another limiting factor of the test was that the organizers placed white pylons in the field as rescue targets, instead of the usual manikins. This created artificial reflectivity that helped Cash’s team find their marks more easily. But, he told DarkSky, his team realized the light could have a problematic effect on actual human subjects — especially anyone lost.

“They’re more likely to go, ‘Oh, look at all this light. Maybe I can find my way back now,’” he said.

Cash was also doubtful it would help SAR helicopter crews locate or navigate at night, noting they already use night vision as standard practice.

His takeaway: For the most part, it’s better to leave the daylight in the daytime.

“It’s not going to make a huge difference to wilderness search and rescue except in a few cases,” Cash told the Chronicle. “We’re pretty good at working at night already.”

Officials’ minds remain open to the technology. A spokesperson for the Kern County Sheriff’s Office told Backpacker that they intend to let SAR groups lead the way on any future implementation. That’s particularly the case with a team like China Lake MRG, a nonprofit that supports its equipment bills mainly through fundraising.

“Given the circumstances, if we needed it, it would definitely be something we would utilize,” said Lori Meza, Kern County Public Information Officer. “But we have no idea what it would cost.”

That price tag could be a real issue. While Reflect Orbital has not officially published any pricing information for its prospective services, Sky & Telescope reported that the company plans to charge around $5,000 per hour for its services, with constellations of multiple satellites — necessary to recreate the kind of bright conditions the team experienced — costing far more. (By comparison, an Airbus H125 helicopter, like the Kern County Sheriff’s Office operates, costs about $2,500 an hour to run, according to charter operator Craft.)

“Most search and rescue teams, at least in California, will not be able to afford it,” Cash told DarkSky. “They won’t even think about affording it. We cannot pay for that sort of thing.”

Emphasizing that any cost was still unknown, Meza said Kern County officials only buy equipment for SAR teams in limited cases. She gave the example of a purchase of vehicles several years ago.

“We do provide certain resources, and there might be an isolated case here or there,” she said. “But a purchase like a contracted service would be a new thing.”

She added that the Sheriff’s Office isn’t taking a position on the technology itself, and that they were grateful to be part of the test.

Other observers are less neutral. DarkSky International has functioned as a sort of public watchdog on Reflect Orbital, lobbying for what it sees as responsible development. Its five principles of outdoor lighting have been widely adopted, and published by the U.S. Department of Energy.

DarkSky sees the same gap in the technology for rescue as China Lake MRG — usefulness, which is also principle one of five.

“The first question is ‘is it needed?’” said Drew Reagan, DarkSky senior marketing and communications manager. “And in this case, the rescue groups found the need was pretty minimal.”

Many of the potential impacts of Reflect’s plan are unknown, because the government has allowed it to move forward with no environmental review.

If its plans become reality, the change in the night environment could be massive. The Federal Communications Commission has given the green light for Reflect to launch its first functional satellite. Its launch plan would place 50,000 in orbit by 2035. Even if they weren’t de facto floodlights, Reagan pointed out, the reflectivity of that many satellites alone would noticeably brighten the night sky everywhere on Earth.

“This represents a fundamentally new technology that will have a direct impact. Not only for the dark sky community or astrotourists, but for migratory birds, for insects,” Reagan said. “It’s not DarkSky’s goal to plunge the world into medieval darkness. We want to be part of these conversations. We just want to make sure we look before we launch.”

The Appalachian Trail: ECT Week 9

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The day has finally come to wrap up the International Appalachian Trail. It has definitely been a unique experience though there was a lot more road walking than I would have preferred. For now, as stage one in my grand journey I will appreciate it for what it was. I’m up and out of camp by 5:40am. Though today will be shorter I have made it my goal to get into the town of Millinocket before the post office closes at 4pm to grab a box my parents sent me. All that stands in my way is about 20 miles of hiking and a 40 minute drive into town. I am most worried about that drive into town. The trailhead where I will pop out after my hike is a bit out of the way though any cars that pass by will only have one option to go into town. There just has to be cars that pass by in the first place.

Today begins with a series of exposed rocky sections. At the top of a few it becomes a bit confusing where to continue on as the cairn stones are very tiny, but on one of these sections I am granted my first view of the backside of Katahdin. Mama K. I am getting so close to the Appalachian Trail now. Not much stands in my way. Hopefully in a day’s time I will be standing atop of Katahdin getting ready to begin my journey along one of the oldest hiking footpaths. I cannot overstate how much I have been dreaming and looking forward to finally starting this trail so seeing the mountain on which it begins has me feeling very emotional. Even though I have 1,600 miles under my belt so far on this journey the IAT was just the warm up. The AT is the main event which will have me, Lord willing, standing atop Springer Mountain in Georgia 2,200 miles away as a triple crowner: a backpacker who has hiked the AT, PCT, and CDT. For now, that seems a long ways away so I just focus on getting into Millinocket on time.

I have one river ford to deal with before making my way along a series of long overgrown and neglected old forest service roads. I make good time until I am confronted by a juvenile moose. I try slowly encroaching into his area to get him to push on, and he runs away. I turn the corner to find him looking over his shoulder with what can only be interpreted as a “now what?” face as he hides behind his much larger mother who is fully facing me and staring me down. Alright, you win. Take your time. I’ll just be here taking a video of you. Wait. Come back. If you ever want wildlife to get going just pull out your phone to try and record them. They always seem like they thwart any plans to get a cool video.

Another half hour has me standing at the intersection of three roads where the IAT ends. No cool sign or terminus. Just one last blaze I take a picture next to. Well, that’s over. Add it to the list of trails I have completed. I have had time to digest the trail since finishing, and I have to say it is probably my least favorite trail I have completed. I don’t hate it, and I am happy to have the experience of hiking it. Though, too often, I found myself wondering what I was doing while walking along a thin road shoulder as cars zoomed by. If you are out there to find a true nature experience it will be few and far between on this one. Then when you are on trail for the most part it’s fairly swampy. Except for Quebec. Quebec had the best of everything. Great trail, great huts, great national parks, great food. Anyone looking to get a taste of Canada via the IAT should just do that 400 mile section.

I’m not trying to be too negative. I really enjoyed the people I met along the way. I enjoyed walking into and out of pretty much every town for resupply. In fact, I only ever had to hitchhike to the northern terminus in Newfoundland and into Charlottetown, PEI. I even enjoyed some of those lonely stretches of road which probably hadn’t seen a car come through in quite some time. Frequently, I would get the impression I was discovering some long lost fishing village, or that I was the first person to step foot in a place for quite some time. I like that feeling of being on the outskirts of civilization looking around with fresh eyes. You tend to notice more.

Mostly, I will miss the water. They don’t call some of those provinces the maritimes for nothing. I will miss having the steady presence of the sea. In the beginning in Newfoundland, I looked out at the horizon and wondered how far away Greenland was (turns out about 800 miles as the crow flies). I would see icebergs floating by. The constant presence of fishing boats going about their business, and the gulls that would circle by looking for scraps. Many of the roads I walked along would be littered with lobster traps and other fishing paraphernalia. Oh, and the seafood! One could get spoiled by the amount of seafood I consumed.

But I left all that behind when I turned inland in Quebec. I will have to walk a pretty far ways down into Florida before all that returns. For now, I have the mountains and the green tunnel of forest that surrounds them. I have an odd 14 miles or so of no man’s land between the end of the IAT and the start of the AT. During this section I walk along Katahdin Lake and marvel at the beauty of one of the shelters there. It has canoes at the shelter for people to use during their stay. Another few miles finds me waving in and out of dense overgrown trail following a series of flags and cairns. At this point, I am so used to poorly marked trail I don’t even slow my pace as I weave in and out, up and around as if I know perfectly where I am going. It seems to work as I reach an intersection with a nice, wide, easy two track. You can always tell you are getting close to the parking lot when things become incredibly easy. Also, when you start seeing more trash.

Katahdin Lake in the background. Maine has quite a few lakes to enjoy.

Here, I befriend an older gentleman who is just finishing up some volunteer trail work. We walk together for a bit before I say my goodbyes and try to push onwards to the trailhead parking lot. Well, I should have just hiked with him as he comes wandering out of the trail as I am still trying to hitch and offers me a ride into town. It is 3pm and it takes about 40 minutes to drive to town. The post office closes at 4pm. I swear it is always a race against time with the post office on trail. Luckily, I make it with 15 minutes to spare. He then drops me off at the hostel where I check in.

Thanks to the AT Hostel for all their help. A great place to stay.

I’m staying at the Appalachian Trail Hostel for two nights. I have worked it out so I will get a shuttle back into Baxter State Park in the morning, climb up Katahdin via the backside, traverse the Knife’s Edge, summit Katahdin and start the AT, summit Hamlin Peak (my first AT side quest), then descend down the far side and get picked back up and taken into town. The next day I will get dropped off where I left off. I will then attempt to hitch to the west side of Baxter State Park to climb North Brother mountain and then hitch back again and continue on to the start of the 100 mile wilderness. These two side quests have been stressing me out for a while. A large part of me wants to attempt to do all of this in a day and not go back into Millinocket, but I know that would be a very long day both in miles and especially in elevation. I think I better play it safer in the middle of a large thru hike.

So I do just that. I play it safe. Though after everything is said and done I think I could definitely have done it as one big day. Going up the backside of Katahdin was fairly easy. I would say it is easier than what northbound hikers have to do on the side of the mountain I descended. The Knife’s Edge wasn’t too exposed save for one short saddle section where I had to descend and then climb back up a steep 40 feet. There was one easy rock climbing move to do that would have been disastrous if I fell. The whole time I traversed the Knife’s Edge it was a complete white out. Finally, I got to Katahdin, Baxter Peak. The start of the AT. I got my picture taken at the famous sign and ate some snacks up there, but even though it is only August it got pretty cold so after 30 minutes I decided to make my way down.

Unfortunately, it was still pretty foggy at the top of Baxter Peak.

Exactly one mile into the AT I turned off to start my side quest of Hamlin Peak. This was a two hour detour which involved going down and then up a saddle, summiting, and then going back down and up the saddle. Fun times. With that taken care of I continued my way down the mountain. There were a lot of sections with rebar drilled into the mountain as makeshift ladders. It was pretty steep. This is definitely the harder side. The sun attempted to come out at times, and by the time I made my way down the mountain it was actually a pretty nice day. I’ll have to come back sometime for better views from the top. I made it to Katahdin Stream Campground just in time to get a shuttle back to Millinocket.

The next day I shuttled back to where I left off except now I will have to try and hitch to the West side of Baxter State Park where North Brother resides. This side quest proved lengthy in time as I stood out there for an hour trying to hitch. Once I got picked up it was about a twenty minute drive. The actual hike was three hours round trip with another hour of trying to get a hitch and twenty minutes of driving back to Katahdin Stream Campground. With that six hour detour finished I continued along where I made it out of Baxter State Park to Abol Bridge Campground. Here I picked up a few items at the store and bought a very large ice cream cone. It was getting late so I hiked another three miles into the 100 Mile Wilderness and called it a night at the first shelter, Hurd Brook Lean-to. I’m not getting very far these first two days so I’m going to have to pick up the pace tomorrow.

I woke up fairly early the next day to get my miles in. I was on trail at 5:40am. The 100 Mile Wilderness is a famous section of the AT though I was surprised when I actually hiked it. Due to its name, it conjures up thoughts of pristine wilderness without any signs of civilization for miles and miles on end. While it might be true that you are far away from civilization, access to civilization and all of its allure still runs heavily through this section. There are some great hostels in the area that will slack pack you for a price. Slack packing is essentially when they hold onto your backpack and you hike your mileage for that day with just a day bag or just the essential gear. They will then pick you up at an agreed upon spot, and take you back to the hostel for a night of town food and a bed. There are many spots along the AT that do this, but here in the 100 Mile Wilderness it seems especially noticeable. There are even packages that can be purchased where you do this over ten days with a meal and bed every night. Such is the fame of the 100 mile wilderness that it brings in all types seeking to “conquer” it. Or at the very least be chauffeured through. Add onto this, there are many active logging roads to be crossed where logging trucks zoom by and the 100 Mile Wilderness is no longer the peaceful serene experience you might imagine. In fact, it is quite popular and crowded to the point where the mice have got in on the act. My first night’s campsite I had a visitor who chewed up a Butterfingers wrapper I had in a pocket. Fortunately, no damage was done to my pack, but this is fairly rare if you’re not staying at a shelter. I guess the campsite was too established.

Caution, unless of course, you want to pay for every luxury.

The 100 Mile Wilderness is beautiful. I can see why people flock to it even when they’re not doing the whole AT. While I did get a heavy taste of rain during my stay, the Maine wilderness is stunning. I’ve never backpacked in Maine before despite visiting many times. The forest is enchanted. There is moss and mushrooms everywhere. Many lakes dot the landscape. And I have arrived during blueberry season. I find at the top of every mountain there are more berries than I can pick. And I try. Believe me I try.

Another great place on the AT. I hope to be back someday.

They run a tight ship at Shaw’s. The owners, Hippie Chick and Poet, have everything down such that, upon being given the tour and asked if any questions, I am just left speechless as to how they distilled all the information I needed so clearly, concisely, and quickly. I was also given a free soda and ice cream sandwich upon arrival which was nice. One of the employees took me to the store later where I bought a very large pizza, jug of chocolate milk, and other goodies for resupply. I was very full. Shaw’s also has a gear store I perused with a nicely curated collection of backpacking gear including some more niche brands you cannot get online or through other big retailers. The one bummer is right before leaving I left my AT branded Darn Tough sleep socks somewhere in the room where I stayed, and by the time I realized and had service to call and see if they were still there several days later someone had swiped them. That was a darn tough pill to swallow as they were very new and those socks cost $28 as they have a lifetime warranty. You can’t really use that warranty without said socks in hand, however.

Famous stack of blueberry pancakes at Shaw’s.

The morning of leaving Shaw’s I had their famous breakfast before hitting trail again. Eggs, bacon, toast, and all you can eat blueberry pancakes. You will leave satisfied. I finish my last day of this week setting myself up for the Kennebec River crossing tomorrow. This is the only river along the AT wide enough that you must get ferried across. The Appalachian Trail Conservancy actually pays someone to ferry hikers across all day in season. The canoe even has a white blaze on it. After this it will truly be all hiking until Key West.

Maine has been such a treat to walk so far. I am excited for more enchanted forest.

Hi, I'm Chris aka Side Quest on trail. While backpacking has been a part of my life since I was little, I began my thru-hiking journey in 2019 by hiking the Walker's Haute Route from Chamonix, France to Zermatt, Switzerland. Since then I have hiked over 8,000 miles including the Pacific Crest and Continental Divide Trails amongst others. In 2026 I will embark on my longest journey yet by walking from Newfoundland to Key West on the Eastern Continental Trail and completing my Triple Crown!

Rank Trackers Find Google Results About One Word Of A Query via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: State Of Agentic AEO For 2027: What’s Scaling & What’s Stalling

Two rank trackers' data shows unrelated Google results that follow one word of the query, mostly below the top spots. Here's how to check a ranking drop.

In mid-September, most unrelated results discovered by SE Ranking using Google rank checking were only about one word of the original search term. Almost all of them started at position 9 or worse.

SE Ranking released an analysis today based on 10,000 Google US desktop keywords it tracks for research purposes. Rank tracking firm Nozzle documented a related pattern in an Oct. 22 report on what it describes as Google ignoring part of a query. According to Nozzle’s findings, page 2 started dropping words on Sept. 16 while page 1 dropped words four days later (Sept. 20). Both firms sell rank tracking software and track ranks through their respective products.

On Oct. 23 I covered how rank tracking providers say they handle the altered Google results; prior to that, on Oct. 14 Roger Montti reported Nozzle’s initial analysis. Both data sets show that most changes occurred in positions below the top positions. Nozzle’s data shows that most of the changes it saw occur on page 2. As such, when reviewing your late September/October ranking reports, check drops below the top positions and on page 2 first.

The primary example of a single-word result set offered by SE Ranking is for the search term “business bank line of credit.” The top 9 results on this search were all financial institutions (banks/lenders) such as Chase Bank, PNC Bank and LendingTree. Results found on page 2 were unrelated to banking or lending as they included videos of music, the LINE messaging application and The Line Hotel; therefore these results only used the word “line” in relation to the original query, rather than answering the query.

In 84% of the cases SE Ranking flagged, the unrelated listings were about one word of the query. The rest followed two or more words. Single-word keywords in its data never came back with unrelated results, which the company says could be because there was no other word for the results to follow.

In one of Nozzle’s examples, “easy strawberry jam” on Sept. 30, the AI Overview still gave a jam recipe while the organic results were about the word “easy,” including the TV series Easy. Nozzle found the page title still showed the full query on 99.8% of the pages that dropped words, with no “Showing results for” notice.

SE Ranking saved up to 50 organic results per keyword, and none of the groups of unrelated results it found started in the top five. They started at positions 6 to 8 in 3.5% of affected result sets and at position 9 or lower in the other 96.5%. SE Ranking notes that a quick look at the top results could make such a page seem fine.

Since Sept. 16, page 2 ignored part of the query on 12.6% of the desktop searches Nozzle could judge on both pages, compared with 2.2% for page 1. On 93.6% of the page 2s that dropped words, page 1 still answered the query.

The way SE Ranking and Nozzle assess a search issue differs, therefore their rates cannot be compared directly to one another.

On Sept. 16, the estimated percentage of keywords in SE Ranking’s sample whose saved result list included any unrelated listing was 0.09%. On Sept. 17 the estimate was 57.3%, and by Sept. 24, which was the end of the test period, the percentage had grown to 67.2%.

Nozzle will flag a search results page if a term that has historically appeared in search results for the keyword appears in 20% or less of those results. Using this method, Nozzle measured that page 1 or page 2 lost terms on approximately 13.9% of the desktop searches it judged on both pages from Sept. 16 through Oct. 21. The highest rate of losing terms was approximately 39.9% on Sept. 28. Nozzle also observed short-term spikes in the occurrence of the pattern since March.

Nozzle viewed this as a different failure type from the “poisoned” search results it reported in October where the query is still answered; however the keyword’s typical websites do not appear. According to Nozzle’s data, when a page lost terms, the keyword’s subsequent search page(s) within 3 days provided an answer to the query about 85.8% of the time.

The numbers shown in SE Ranking’s daily report spanned from Sept. 17 through Sept. 24. SE Ranking’s baseline tests run in October utilized differing collection settings. A sampling of cases was assessed by two independent AI reviewers, and 40 disputed cases were manually reviewed. SE Ranking adds that its data only covers organic listings and can’t tell Google’s intent or what searchers saw, and that Google hasn’t publicly explained the results.

SE Ranking notes that if an unrelated result has taken over the lower part of a search engine results page (SERP), a website which normally appears there may appear to be missing from a tracker report without its rank necessarily changing for people using the Google search engine.

Before reporting a late September/October drop at position 9 or lower or on a second page, go through the collected results from your last update to see if the pages that took your place were related to just one word in the query. Then go back to your next update and look at Search Console data for impressions and clicks on the same query, as SE Ranking suggests.

AccuRanker, whose page 1 history check I described on Oct. 23, has updated its post to say pages 2 and beyond have a related problem, with results for only one word of the query. It says it will cover that in a separate article. Check with your tracker to see whether it reviews page 2 or stops at page 1.

A European night against an in-form team: Torriani gears up for biggest opportunity yet

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

AC Milan will take on the Europa League table-toppers Ferencvaros without their captain Mike Maignan, which presents an opportunity.

Milan do not have long to enjoy their impressive 3-1 derby victory against Inter because the match against Ferencvaros is scheduled for 18:45 CET at San Siro on Thursday. The Hungarian side are in excellent form, too.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

They are third in their domestic league with 20 points – just two behind top-seeded Gyor, and with a game in hand. They are fourth in the Europa League with nine points out of nine as well, thanks to wins against Torreense, Plzen and Celtic.

This will therefore not be an easy match for Milan, who are down in 24th place after defeats to Benfica and Bournemouth either side of a win against RB Salzburg.

Speaking of the Bournemouth game, Maignan’s red card in that means he must serve a one-match suspension. A multi-game ban was feared but the Frenchman will be available for the match against Olympiacos, scheduled for November 26th.

For the match against the Hungarians, Lorenzo Torriani will be between the posts. This is a great opportunity for a player who came through the youth academy, and one in whom the club and Ruben Amorim have great faith.

After making his debut on 17 September 2024 in the 3-1 Champions League defeat to Liverpool, he has so far played just 141 minutes with the first team. To date Torriani is yet to play his first match in Italy’s top flight, Serie A.

In fact, in addition to the aforementioned match against Liverpool, the Italian has only started in the Rossoneri’s 6-1 victory over Sassuolo in the Coppa Italia in December 2024, and in several pre-season friendlies. So, this will be his first start in a ‘top’ competition for his boyhood team.

Buttons are back and storage is king when it comes to car interiors

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

We talk with Nissan designer Hiren Patel about how vehicle interior designs are changing.

As with interior design for houses, interior design for vehicles is also subject to fashion trends. And like any fashion trend, even the best ideas can become stale and played out with overuse. Some ideas weren’t the best, either, like demonstrably worse human-machine interfaces that add to driver distraction; yet, they still proliferated throughout the industry and across the cars offered for sale. But the good news is that the pushback seems to be working.

“When the trend of the Piano Black and the switchless interior was happening, it was during a time when designers and companies and engineers and everybody were thinking that, ‘Hey, we can just get rid of all these switches on the interior. We can put it all in the software. It’ll save money. It’ll save us tooling up all these extra parts for unique cars and all this stuff and make the interior more like a software experience.’ And it was done with those intentions,” said Hiren Patel, senior design manager at Nissan.

That resulted in cars with minimalist interiors that looked good in the dealership. But people drive their cars on roads; they don’t just sit inside them, parked inside a dealership. “So lots of people were complaining about it and saying, ‘Hey, it takes my eyes off the road to change the temperature or to adjust my seat or do whatever.’ It’s not as easy as doing something on your phone where it’s just right at your fingertips and intuitive,” Patel said.

In part, blame the increasing complexity of modern cars, which have multiple subsystems that need to be controlled. “It’s really like I’ve got to go in all these menus and do stuff, and all the car companies have a different way of doing it, too. So maybe nobody’s really figured out a great way of doing it.”

Most recently, Patel was responsible for the new Rogue, Nissan’s bestselling family SUV. And its interior is a good one. Less minimalist than the all-electric Ariya, the new Rogue Hybrid has buttons and switches for most of its controls and a useful amount of storage built into the interior. Yet it’s not a boring design—the seats are a standout, taking inspiration from puffer jackets. And no, there’s no Piano Black in sight.

“People want easy, intuitive controls,” Patel said. “They want wireless charging. They want surfaces that are easy to manage and clean, keep clean and clutter-free, and they want lots of places to store things. Oh, and the last thing is, they want easy access.” So the new Rogue’s rear doors were engineered to open to 90˚ to make it easier to put in a kid’s car seat or help an elderly parent into the back. That would be less of a priority for a sedan being marketed at a more image-conscious customer, but vitally important for this segment.

“The target customer for this product… they have families and they have a lot of stuff. So you’ll notice that you open the door, and immediately there’s places to put stuff,” Patel said.

Utilitarian need not mean prosaic, however. As designers since the Bauhaus movement have proved, “everyday” doesn’t have to be ordinary. Patel shared that sentiment. “One of the exciting things and one of the really big challenging things of doing a car or doing design for a product that is a usable everyday product is when you can make that product beautiful,” he said. “There’s so much more that goes into our everyday usable products in terms of cost, engineering, usability, feasibility, everything. And then on top of that, we’re struggling and really trying our best to fight for every little millimeter that we can get that we feel is important to making sure that that product is appealing from a visual standpoint.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

3 Key Takeaways From Lagos Fashion Week

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Africa’s biggest and buzziest fashion week drew to a close on Sunday night with an intimate dinner held by Nigerian heavyweight designer Lisa Folawiyo, who returned to the official Lagos Fashion Week schedule after a seven-year break. The five-day event saw approximately 16,000 people in attendance, up from 15,000 last season. Some 65 designers flocked to the Nigerian metropolis to showcase their collections to an audience of press, influencers, and other industry figures.

This season, it was local talent who dominated the week, with several Nigerian designers showcasing their collections on and off-schedule. As with all Lagos Fashion Week events, shows happen on-site at the Federal Palace Hotel, while off-site shows take place around the city. Mainstay designers like Emmy Kasbit, Ugo Monye, and Sisano showcased their collections through a mix of presentations and runway shows. Other buzzy designers such as Hertunba welcomed guests to their new stores (in addition to their runway shows), while LFJ held her Spring/Summer 2027 runway at her concept store Akoko.

Even those who now show up at fashion week internationally like Iamisigo, who showcased her collection in Copenhagen, and Orange Culture and Fruché, who both presented their collections in Berlin, are committed to returning and being a part of the Lagos Fashion Week calendar, too.

Now in its 16th edition, the platform is gaining more international attention than ever before. International buyers and media were in town from the Middle East as well as neighboring African countries including Senegal and Ghana, while buzzy US influencers and models Eni Popoola and Alton Mason also flew in for the shows.

(Left) Jermaine Bleu, and Boyedoe (right).

During the week, guests gathered at the US General Consulate to witness Omoyemi Akerele, founder of Lagos Fashion Week, receive an Excellence in Commercial and Creative Leadership Award from Consul General Brandon Hudspeth. Guests included Chioma Ude, founder of Africa International Film Festival, influencers Popoola and Akin Faminu, as well as designers like Ejiro Amos Tafiri. The award signifies Lagos Fashion Week’s ability to help springboard talent into the international market, particularly in the US.

“[Akerele] has built a platform that brings Nigerian fashion to the world,” said Consul General Hudspeth during Friday’s event. “American talent has found a home right here in Lagos. Last year, singer Ciara was right here making headlines on the Lagos runway during fashion week.” He also points to brands like US’s Tia Adeola and Studio 189, a label co-founded by actress Rosario Dawson, which have shown on the Lagos schedule.

Omoyemi Akerele, founder of Lagos Fashion Week receiving an award from Brandon Hudspeth, the Consul General.

Beyond the runway, Nigerian designers are going global, Hudspeth acknowledged. “Nigerian designers are reaching American customers, and that creates business for Americans and Nigerians,” he added, pointing to New York-based e-commerce platform Moda Operandi, which holds digital trunk shows for Lagos Fashion Week designers, and trade shows like Coterie, which has featured local talent. “This, of course, is cultural diplomacy, but it is also commercial diplomacy, and the consulate is working to build these relations and connections.”

African designers who have spent more than two decades in the industry returned to the schedule, including Christie Brown and Lisa Folawiyo, as well as Bridget Awosika from Nigeria. “I feel it’s important that we were within the official calendar. It makes sense,” said Folawiyo. “It’s something I really thought about and I felt this is the right time.” The brand, which turned 21 years old this year, brought Lagos Fashion Week to a close with a candle lit five-course meal, surrounded by an intimate crowd of celebrities, influencers, friends and industry leaders.

For Ghana’s Christie Brown, returning to Lagos Fashion Week after an eight-year hiatus was part of a larger expansion strategy as the brand builds its presence across the continent. “As much as we’re building a global brand, we also do believe that there are certain key markets on the continent that we absolutely need to have a presence in,” said founder and creative director Aisha Ayensu. Those hotspots include Kenya, Côte d’Ivoire, Nigeria, Ghana, and South Africa. “This is a re-entry into the market. We don’t want to come in guns blazing. We want to enter cautiously.” The brand now has a six-month residency at luxury retailer Temple Muse, giving local consumers an opportunity to shop Christie Brown pieces in Lagos.

Ugo Monye, the founder of his eponymous label, returned after a year-long break. The popular Nigerian designer explained that there’s an overwhelming pressure to create and prioritize design. Many simply enjoy the design process and do not pay attention to whether the backend business operations are in order. Unlike in the West, where designers are creating collections for four or more seasons, Monye and several African designers prefer to do one collection a year. He said his collections from two seasons ago are still in demand.

“Because I was having some backend issues, the workload was a lot and then to add fashion week on top of it, I just said, ‘You know what? Let me just take a break and catch my breath, then come back,’” he said, adding that he is the only person on his team overseeing the business. “Our industry is not fully structured yet, so you have to be a part of everything, even when you hire people.”

Despite the break, Monye is committed to showcasing in Lagos. “I’m not going anywhere. I’m here for a long time,” he said. “The brand [leaving] the fashion week calendar is out of the question.”

Showing during Lagos Fashion Week is increasingly important because of the rising media attention, said Abelia Anaba Franklyn, founder of couture house Weiz Dhurm Franklyn. “[Designers] want more coverage. It’s such an important moment in fashion, African fashion and Nigerian fashion,” she said. “Nigerians have the spending power. Once you have a distinct point of view that is not a regurgitation of another brand, people will acclimate toward you.” Now, the couturier is branching further into ready-to-wear where her pieces start from $600.

Elsewhere, others used fashion week as an opportunity to announce major business news. On Saturday, Post-Imperial shared that it was shutting down after 10 years. “I’ve said everything I needed to say with Post-Imperial,” said founder Niyi Okuboyejo backstage at his final show, which was a collaboration with Nigerian sustainable fashion brand This Is Us. Over the past decade, the brand built a strong wholesale model and was in over 200 retail stores, from Bergdorf Goodman in the US to Alára in Nigeria.

“Today’s a great day for me,” he added. “I also tried to expand the idea of what fashion is, and fashion shows aren’t necessarily just runway.” With this final collection, held in the courtyard, Okuboyejo gathered friends of the brands to walk the makeshift runway, including stylist Ugo Mozie, industrial designer Nifemi Marcus-Bello, and Dada Gallery’s Oyinkan Dada.

There’s often a misconception that Lagos Fashion Week is just for Nigerian designers. While they make up the majority, there are a handful of designers from across the continent who traveled to cash in on the buzz. This season saw some new and old international designers join the schedule, including Ghana’s Christie Brown and IGC Fashion from Uganda.

Unable to secure sponsorships in time, IGC Fashion self-funded its trip to Lagos Fashion Week, hoping to connect with media, a new audience and a larger fashion market. “We’re lacking media and big investors in the country. It’s a big opportunity for designers in Uganda to be at a stage like Lagos Fashion Week,” said brand founder Katende Godfrey.

Over in North Africa, Moroccan designer Scheherazade Essrhir shares a similar sentiment. After participating in Lagos Fashion Week’s Green Access, an incubator for young designers, Essrhir took some time away to perfect her craft. “Through Green Access, I learnt that being a designer is more than creating and designing. It’s a lot of work,” she said. “[At Lagos Fashion Week], they support us even when the show is over.” In Lagos, Essrhir feels she has support and community as the fashion week team schedules regular check-in calls and feedback.

From a business lens, South African designer Mzukisi Mbane, founder of Imprint ZA, believes Lagos is the place to be. “In South Africa, we have fashion shows. We have the glitz and the glamour. But for me, the bottom line is always important,” he said. “I want to be visible to people that can help the brand grow and be seen… even if [buyers] are not purchasing pieces, I’m still on their radar.” Mbane was named a recipient of this year’s Africa Fashion Up Award, an initiative dedicated to nurturing emerging regional designers, and will see him receive mentorship from Balenciaga, since the Kering-owned brand has on-going partnership with the platform.

British Nigerian designer Reni Abina chose to bring her collection to Lagos again this season. “As we expand internationally, it’s important to me that we remain connected to the city and the community that first embraced us,” Abina said. “We’ve always wanted to build a truly global fashion brand, but I don’t believe that global recognition should come at the expense of our roots.”

There were a handful of buyers in attendance this season, many of whom noticed a shift in the overall collections on display. “I’ve been coming [to Lagos Fashion Week] for a few years, and this is probably the most cohesive year that I’ve ever experienced,” says Arinola Fagbemi, a buyer at Lagos-based concept store Alara, adding that they are now building collections around who their client is and what they might want to buy from them.

Wearability is a key concern. This season, Alara partnered up with Lagos Fashion Week to host a showroom for designers after their runway shows. It’s a move that’s really helped buyers, including the Alara team, see the clothes in-person and gauge things like finishing, texture, and weight. Standout shows for her include Orange Culture, Dukun, and Orire. “We’re looking for the finishing. Clients are very discerning, especially the Alara client. They want things that are well made. They don’t care that it’s from Nigeria or from Africa. They expect the same level of detail,” she says.

African retailers are setting aside a separate budget for Lagos. “We’ve understood now that it works differently,” says Fagbemi, adding they do not follow the international buying calendar and place orders for every season. “The fortunate thing is that the turnaround time for a lot of African brands is very quick. So what we’re seeing on the runway now, many of them will have it ready for the holidays, because of Detty December [a festive period full of parties and live music events] and that peak period.” Alara buyers also attend Milan, London and Paris fashion weeks.

Viva Accra operates on a different model. “We have an allocation for African designers within our overall buying budget, rather than a completely separate budget for Lagos. It’s about finding the right brands and making considered buying decisions,” says Sacha Okoh, creative director and buyer at Viva Accra, a concept store in Ghana.

Lagos Fashion Week is an important discovery platform. “We have a genuine interest in growing our African designer offering,” continues Okoh, noting that the store currently carries a number of Nigerian and Ghanaian brands, but she is “always looking for designers whose work I feel can sit comfortably alongside our international labels.”

She adds: “A collection can be beautiful on the runway, but for me, it’s also about how those pieces translate into real wardrobes. I look for that balance between creativity, quality, wearability and commercial appeal.” Standout collections include Christie Brown, Jermaine Bleu and Boyedoe. Okoh regularly attends fashion weeks and trade shows in Milan, Paris, and London.

As Sunny Dolat, a researcher, consultant and Lagos Fashion Week SS27’s creative director, observes: “This year, the platform attracted an even larger international audience than last, with many visitors traveling to Nigeria specifically for fashion week,” he says. “This strongly indicates a platform that is not only well regarded and respected, but has firmly established itself as an important destination for discovering some of the most compelling design talent from across the African continent.”

Here’s How to Train From Now Through the New Year to Maintain Your Hard-Earned Marathon Fitness

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A run coach and an exercise physiologist break down the weekly mileage and workouts you need to preserve your speed, strength, and endurance.

You’ve spent months training for your fall marathon, so the thought of losing that hard-earned fitness after race day probably stings. But at the same time, you can’t simply continue logging miles and workouts like you’re in race mode. Trying to stay razor-sharp year-round can lead to injury and burnout; your body needs rest after training for peak fitness.

So, instead, put your marathon fitness on ice. Freeze the adaptations you’ve gained and the endurance you’ve built. Then, you can pick up where you left off on, say, January 1, or whenever it’s time to lock back in for a spring marathon.

If you train right in the weeks between now and the new year, you won’t feel like you’re rebuilding fitness from scratch. Instead, you’ll preserve the key aerobic, muscular, and endurance adaptations you’ve worked so hard to develop.

To do that, we explain the minimum miles you need for maintenance and how speed, strength, and cross-training keep you in prime condition. Expect to return to race training feeling well-rested, confident, and ready to tackle another marathon cycle this spring.

In the first two weeks immediately following your marathon, you’ll want to focus more on rest and easy time-based jogging. Once you’ve recovered, however, you can return to tracking your miles more closely.

The bare minimum mileage you’ll need to maintain will vary from runner to runner, Heather Milton, CSCS, a clinical exercise physiologist and cofounder of Master My Run, tells Runner’s World. Much of it depends on how many miles you regularly put in over the weeks of your marathon training cycle.

“Science does not give us an exact number of what percentage of your marathon volume to decrease to that still allows you to maintain the same amount of aerobic fitness,” Milton explains. From her coaching experience, however, she’s found this number is likely around 50 percent of a runner’s typical weekly mileage.

David Hall, an RRCA- and USATF-certified run coach and founder of Running Coach CT, agrees. When planning his athletes’ schedules for the off-season, he looks at their typical running volume during marathon training and reduces their weekly mileage to no less than half. Every fourth week, however, mileage can drop to as low as 20 percent of their average to help facilitate recovery.

“A runner who was running 60 miles per week during their marathon build could definitely cut that in half,” Milton says. Or, take a runner who averaged around 40 miles per week. This individual could reduce their weekly mileage to 20, add in some cross-training, and expect to maintain much of their progress.

In fact, Milton adds, every runner can benefit from incorporating more of those cross-training sessions in the off-season. “Other forms of exercise can decrease the repetitiveness of the running motion and help you develop more well-rounded fitness, so I do prefer more variety from cross-training in the down-season,” she explains.

At the same time, Milton wants people to do the exercise they enjoy and, of course, runners love running. “I have to be reasonable about what a runner is willing to do and what keeps them healthy while they’re doing it,” she says. If you want to keep running four days per week in the off-season, that’s still workable—with one important caveat: You need to prioritize strength training.

When you’re in the thick of marathon training, running is your main focus. So while you’re still doing strength work during this time, it mostly helps maintain the strength you already have, rather than increase it, Milton explains. The off-season provides an ideal time to really build up and strengthen your muscle mass and bone health as much as possible.

Each week, Milton says to aim for two to three resistance training sessions that focus on heavier weights with a low to moderate number of reps. (You can do more than three weekly sessions as long as you’re not working the same muscle groups two days in a row.) This sets you up for a stronger return to your spring training cycle because it helps improve running economy, your overall neuromuscular health, and your fast-twitch muscle fibers, which help power you through fast bursts of running or hill climbing.

“Plus, when you start training for your next marathon, you’ll be in a stronger position to increase your running volume again because you’ve strengthened all of the areas of your body that were impacted by your previous race, which can help prevent injuries,” Milton adds.

During the off-season, you certainly don’t need to run every day to maintain your fitness. In fact, you can preserve key fitness markers—such as your VO2 max—with as little as three workouts per week.

“The goal is to maintain at least three to four aerobic days per week to maintain your fitness level,” says Milton. You can opt to run as little as once per week, as long as you continue to cross-train on the remaining days, she adds.

Aim for 30 to 60 minutes of whichever aerobic exercise you most enjoy—hit the gym for the elliptical or indoor pool, or get outside for skiing and cycling.

Similarly, the number of running days per week for Hall’s athletes varies, but many have success with three because it allows more time for strength and flexibility work. He also adds a second rest day to the schedules of his runners who typically train six days per week during a marathon block. But in both cases, consistency is crucial—you don’t want to go a week without doing anything. “Do something every other day at a minimum to stay active,” Hall says.

One of your runs each week should be a bit longer than the others, but it doesn’t need to match the lengths of your true marathon long runs. Milton typically recommends a long run between five and 13 miles, depending on the individual’s training volume.

If you don’t want to worry about tracking mileage in the off-season, that’s fine, too. Running for time instead of miles provides the same endurance benefits, Hall says. Around four weeks postmarathon, his athletes will typically start with one-hour long runs. “Then, they’ll go up to 75 minutes, then to 90 minutes, and then maybe up to an hour and 45,” he explains.

Over several weeks, this gradual progression sets his runners up with a solid base to build from when they start their spring marathon training. “But don’t push the pace,” he emphasizes. “These are easy, conversational efforts.”

Whether you run for time or miles, make sure you’re not increasing either too quickly. “Keep the same rules in mind as when you’re building mileage in marathon training,” Milton says. “Aim to increase by no more than 10 percent each week.” Recent research suggests that, to reduce injury risk, you can apply this rule to the distance of any single run, as well.

What does this look like in practice? Let’s say a runner is doing five aerobic sessions per week, which includes about 20 miles of running. Two are cross-training days and the remaining three are running days. They might choose to run three to five miles one day, five to seven miles another day, and then five to 13 miles for their long run. You also don’t need to increase distance as quickly as you would in marathon training, Milton explains.

Over three weeks, she says a runner might balance their mileage as follows:

Week 1: 4 miles, 6 miles, 10 milesWeek 2: 5 miles, 6 miles, 9 milesWeek 3: 3 miles, 7 miles, 11 miles

In the four weeks immediately following your marathon, don’t worry about fast workouts or hitting paces, says Hall. During those first two weeks postrace, he advises his athletes to go for 20- to 30-minute daily walks. In the third and fourth weeks, he reintroduces easy running and a touch of speed.

“The general rule I apply is: no hard running for the number of days that’s equal to the number of miles in the race. So for a marathon, we’re talking about 30 days of walking and conversational effort running—maybe dipping into tempo pace a bit—but certainly nothing above lactate threshold.”

After that time, Hall says runners can start to add back intensity. In fact, he encourages it, suggesting you incorporate either a 20-minute run at threshold pace or an interval session for the same amount of time. He also recommends alternating one mile at your threshold pace, followed by one minute of easy jogging or brisk walking, until you reach 20 minutes.

“Training at lactate threshold pace can make conversational effort feel easier and can help you run faster while still remaining mostly in an aerobic zone,” Hall explains. In other words, your body adapts so running at any pace starts to feel like it requires less work.

Another way to get in some speed during the winter season? Sign up for a holiday race like a Turkey Trot or Jingle Bell Jog, he says. “It can get boring if all you do is train. Racing a 5K keeps the interest alive and lets you put in a hard effort.” Keep in mind, though, you did just run a marathon, so you don’t need to race all out or cross the finish line with a PR.

According to both Hall and Milton, the golden rule that will help you maintain your hard-earned gains is consistency. Once you’ve recovered physically and mentally from 26.2, get back into a routine that keeps you moving. “Doing something is always better than doing nothing,” Hall says—because you can lose 8 percent of your VO2 max in four to six weeks if you pull the plug on activity completely.

Similarly, you’ll want to avoid “stop-and-go” training. “If you’re running two to four days per week, keep running two to four days per week, every week—within reason, because life gets in the way sometimes,” Milton says. But running 30 miles one week, doing nothing for three weeks, and then trying to get back into it can lead to a real struggle, she adds.

As long as you do your best to stay consistent—and don’t stress when the holidays, travel, or other engagements inevitably interfere—you’ll find your fitness quite close to where you left it after your fall marathon. All that’s left for you to do? Thaw it out with a fresh marathon training cycle this spring.

Morgan is a gear editor who has been with Runner’s World since 2017. She started as an intern ghostwriting The Warmup, a bygone version of the daily RW newsletter. Now, she tests and reviews anything you might find on runners’ feet-from crew socks and compression boots to carbon-plated super shoes.

A lifelong runner and shoe geek, Morgan has been chasing the perfect pair of kicks since she joined her grade school cross-country team. Since then, she ran as a Division I walk-on for the cross-country and track & field teams at the University of Delaware, where she studied English and Biology. She has one full marathon under her belt, and has raced more halfs and 5Ks than she can count.

Skip Salmon: 15 More Sustainable Fish Alternatives

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

From Japan to New Jersey, salmon is one of the most in-demand, beloved fish in the world, prized for its rich flavor and succulently flaky flesh. But while it's undeniably delicious, there can be some nuances in how sustainable certain varieties are. Sourcing sustainable alternatives can be tricky, but knowing the right certifications and sources can go a long way toward purchasing the right seafood.

Salmon isn't inherently unsustainable, but an incredible amount of global demand can lead to some less-than-ideal practices. In particular, open-net salmon farms may cause environmental issues, whether that's pollution runoff, unsustainable sources of fish feed, or the spread of diseases and parasites to wild populations. One of the biggest differences between farmed and wild salmon is that wild varieties are often more sustainable, depending on the fishery, and well-managed populations are carefully monitored to ensure long-term viability. Still, New Zealand is famous for its highly rated salmon aquaculture, so it would be wrong to paint all farms with the same brush.

When it comes to sourcing other, more sustainable fish, it's often a solid idea to look for something small and fast-growing rather than something large and predatory. While some certifications may greenwash the sustainability of seafood, guidance from the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration can help consumers identify responsibly managed fisheries. Despite salmon's unique qualities, there are many fish out there that are more affordable and sustainable and can still be high-quality substitutes in your regular diet.

If you don't want to change up textures and taste, char can be a more sustainable fish with a mild flavor and fewer calories. As a salmonid, it's remarkably similar to salmon and has the same pink flesh, marbling, and savory skin you've already come to love.

Few fish species are quite as environmentally friendly as responsibly sourced sardines, since they're small prey fish that grow quite rapidly in their first years of life. Fresh, dried, frozen, or tinned, sardines come in enough varieties that there's an option for just about everyone.

Freshwater fish get a bad rap for being milder and leaner than ocean-dwelling species, and responsibly farmed rainbow trout is about as tasty and environmentally friendly as it gets. A thick, skin-on fillet cooked with a pat of butter can be just as moist and succulent, and modern farming practices can be quite eco-conscious.

Tasting like someone managed to cross the buttery sweetness of lobster with a fish, sablefish, also known as black cod, is an ocean delicacy. Even well-managed farming operations can have a relatively low environmental impact, particularly when they use responsible feed sources and strict measures to prevent disease.

Essentially just a giant perch, barramundi is a flaky, versatile protein you can prepare exactly the same way you'd cook salmon. They're a unique case in that they're quite large but can thrive on feeds containing plant-based ingredients, so responsibly managed commercial farming operations can have a relatively low environmental impact.

A catfish may not be much to look at, but they're certainly a lot to taste. As bottom-feeders, they can thrive on an omnivorous diet that includes plant-based ingredients, and while they might be a touch lean, few proteins are quite as good when deep-fried or bathed in butter.

One of the mildest fish you can use in just about anything, halibut is a largely sustainable choice, particularly when it's wild-caught in the Pacific. Atlantic populations have faced steep declines from overfishing, so be sure to source it appropriately.

When you're looking for something to bread, batter, or mix into a salad, few types of seafood are quite as good as whiting. Like other fish, however, its sustainability depends on its source, and some well-managed fisheries in U.S. Atlantic waters are more sustainable than those targeting depleted populations elsewhere.

While mahi-mahi sourced from Central America and southern Asia can be a bit problematic, those caught in responsibly managed fisheries in the Atlantic can be quite sustainable. While the fish can reach a respectable 3 feet in length, they grow rapidly, and their populations can recover quite quickly with responsible management.

Snapper is incredibly delicious, but of all the various species, yellowtail snapper from well-managed fisheries can be a particularly responsible choice. Historically, government authorities and local fishers work together to manage certain populations, helping ensure that these fish are harvested responsibly.

Huge, sweet, and oh-so-fun to reel in, the greater amberjack is a common sight for saltwater fishers looking to make tacos or cut thick steaks for the grill. Strict controls over harvesting wild-caught fish can help protect vulnerable populations, although sustainability varies by fishery, so it's worth checking the source before buying it at a seafood counter.

Sometimes called sunshine bass, this hybrid fish is a cross between a saltwater striped bass and a freshwater white bass. These fish grow quickly and tolerate a wide range of conditions, making it relatively easy to raise them sustainably with minimal environmental impact.

Eating carp can not only be sustainable, but buying invasive varieties sourced from the American South can actually be a boon for the environment! Several species of invasive carp have spread through U.S. waterways, so every bite of their earthy, slightly tangy flesh can help support efforts to protect these delicate ecosystems.

Blackened red drum, also called redfish, was once so popular that it prompted an outright commercial fishing ban in federal waters of the Gulf of Mexico. While responsibly farm-raised red drum can still be a sustainable choice, along with certain wild-caught black drum, it's generally still best to avoid wild-caught red drum, as sustainability varies by fishery.

Fresh, clean, and a premium delicacy, grouper can be a gray area of sustainability. While many species, like goliath, Nassau, and Gulf groupers, face serious conservation concerns and may be illegal to harvest in certain areas, responsibly sourced black grouper can be a great substitute for premium-grade salmon.

“Scot Will Be There” Parker’s First West Ham Decisions Could Come Within Hours

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Claret & Hugh has been informed within the past few moments that incoming West Ham manager Scott Parker is likely to have some input into team selection for tomorrow night’s Championship game against Cardiff City. The former Hammers midfielder is also expected to attend the match, despite Mark Noble being set to take charge on the touchline.

As West Ham confirmed in their official statement earlier today, Noble, Steve Potts and Gerard Prenderville have been placed in temporary charge of preparations following the dismissal of Nuno Espírito Santo. However, the information we’ve received suggests that Parker could still have a say in the team that faces Cardiff.

A C&H source told us just moments ago: “Scot will be there and he’ll have some influence on the team even if he can’t do all of it.”

West Ham’s final training session ahead of the match took place earlier today at Rush Green, before Parker had even signed his proposed two-and-a-half-year contract. But that doesn’t mean he will have no input at all, particularly with the new manager expected to be in attendance tomorrow evening.

Being something of a Championship specialist, and of course a former Hammer, Parker will already be familiar with West Ham’s form and performances this season. I suspect he’ll also have a reasonable idea of which players he wants in the team and which ones might benefit from a spell on the sidelines.

As Claret & Hugh revealed earlier today, Parker’s proposed contract is closely tied to West Ham securing promotion this season. With that in mind, there is every reason for him to want to influence proceedings as quickly as possible, even if Noble is the man officially overseeing tomorrow’s game.

And, to be perfectly honest, I don’t think Parker needs to reinvent the wheel to make some immediate improvements.

There are a couple of really easy wins available to the new Irons gaffer, and some of them should be blindingly obvious to anybody who has watched West Ham over the past few months.

Morato was Nuno’s first choice left back.

Firstly, a rousing team talk and a little bit of urgency might be enough to address West Ham’s dreadful starts to games. It was a recurring problem under Nuno Espírito Santo, and one the former manager never seemed capable of fixing. Getting the players motivated and ready to compete from the opening whistle would be a welcome change.

Then there’s the fairly straightforward business of putting round pegs in round holes. For starters, let’s play an actual left-back in the left-back position. That alone would immediately improve the balance of the team rather than continuing to ask players to operate in unfamiliar roles.

Finding the right blend in midfield would also be of huge benefit. West Ham have spent far too much time surrendering control of what is arguably the most important area of the pitch, despite having some talented options available. Getting that balance right could make an enormous difference to the team’s performances.

And finally, there’s Jarrod Bowen. Surely one of the most obvious priorities for any incoming West Ham manager is getting our best attacking player into as many goalscoring positions as possible. Bowen has spent far too much time being asked to produce something out of nothing, and a better-functioning team should make his job considerably easier.

Okay, it’s all a bit paint by numbers, and there’s nothing particularly revolutionary or overly tactical about those suggestions. But that’s rather the point.

West Ham’s problems have often appeared so obvious that you wonder why they’ve been allowed to continue for so long. Parker doesn’t necessarily need weeks on the training ground to recognise them, and even a quick word with the players before kick-off might help address some of the most glaring deficiencies.

Of course, Noble will be responsible for managing the team against Cardiff, and there’s only so much Parker can realistically influence before he’s properly taken charge.

But if the new manager can offer a few sensible suggestions and inject a little urgency into proceedings, I’d suggest that would already be a step in the right direction.

Kris Gonzo (commonly known to the West Ham community as Gonzo) is the co-founder of Hammers Chat (established 2014) and the lead video content creator and columnist for Claret and Hugh.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.

A lifelong Hammer since the legendary 10-0 victory over Bury, when he isn't providing daily match analysis on the Hammers Chat YouTube Channel or broadcasting on X (@GONZObignose), he can be found walking his dog and restoring classic pinball machines.

Indie games are missing launch dates because Steam is backed up

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.

One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Many indie game developers have recently posted on social media about unexpectedly long approval wait times for their game submissions on Steam, with some of the approvals causing developers to miss their planned launch dates.

For example, Studio Daimon, the team behind an upcoming title called Food Devils, had planned to launch the game in early access today after submitting its build on October 12th. But the game is “still awaiting approval,” according to a post from game director Alfred Kamon. “Even now, we don’t know when Steam will give us the final OK to finally release our game. We sent another ticket last week saying that the situation was urgent and that we might miss our release date because of this, but it also got ignored.”

In October, publisher 2 Left Thumbs said it missed its planned launch of Wheel Make It “by nearly two weeks” due to the long wait for build reviews. You can read some details about the studio’s setbacks, including failing a review for a “one single option in the menu” not being accessible with a controller, in a Steam post. There are lots of other recent posts about review build waits as well.

Valve says on a Steam developer website that a review of a game’s store presence “typically takes 3-5 business days to complete,” though the company suggests submitting a page for review “at least 7 business days before you want it live to account for potential changes you’ll need to make.”

However, as of late, Steam has seemingly been grappling with a sharp increase in games; October’s Steam Next Fest had 7,240 games, a 65.2 percent increase over June’s Steam Next Fest, according to How to Market a Game. A Reddit user also found that 49.9 percent of all games released since Valve started asking developers in 2024 to label games that use AI now have an AI disclosure.

Kamon and 2 Left Thumbs suspect the longer waits are due to “slop” slowing down the process. “We can only assume it’s because Steam’s review queue is clogged up by AI slop, but the whole process is now taking several weeks for some developers,” said Kamon.

In a new post from Valve published after we first ran this story, the company acknowledged that following a “a significant increase in submissions for store and page review in recent months,” it’s “running a little behind right now” and taking “up to 12 business days” for reviews, saying that much of the delay is “having to re-review games.” It has updated its release checklist for developers, including requiring them to run a game through Steam for at least five minutes. Valve also says that further improvements are on the way for the build review process.

Itch.io has also been dealing with a rise in games, with the platform saying last weekend that “The number of game pages being created per-day has doubled in the past 2 months.” Last night, the platform said it “Just deleted a couple terabytes of ai slop 😌”

Update, November 3rd: Added new post from Valve.

Apple Is Putting an Age Limit on Falling in Love With AI

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Dating and AI companion apps have new age gate restrictions, while apps that share your location will have to disclose it.

Practicing flirting using Replika's AI system.

Falling in love with artificial intelligence raises a lot of questions about loneliness, human connection and what the heck we’re doing as a species. Apple has a more immediate concern: Are you at least 18?

The company is tightening its App Store rules, requiring dating apps and AI companions that offer romantic or sexual interactions to carry an 18-and-over age rating. The change applies whether you’re looking for love with another human being or flirting with a chatbot that never leaves you on read.

In a notice to developers on Monday, Apple said it will introduce two new content descriptors on Nov. 16: “Dating and Relationships” and “Shared Location.” Developers will have to disclose whether their apps offer dating features, including romantic or sexual interactions with virtual companions, or whether they allow users to share their real-world locations with others.

Location-sharing features may also affect age ratings, depending on the country. In Brazil, for example, apps that allow users to share their locations must carry a minimum age rating of 16, while dating apps require an 18-and-over rating. Developers have until Jan. 12, 2027, to complete the new disclosures for all their apps.

Most major dating apps, including Tinder and Hinge, already require users to be at least old enough to vote (in the US, that is). The more interesting part is how Apple’s rules could affect AI companions, which have become increasingly popular.

Last year, Character.AI, a platform where users can create and interact with fictional AI personalities, removed open-ended chats for users under 18. The company continued offering other features to younger users, but the decision came amid growing concerns about the emotional and psychological risks of forming relationships with AI.

A 2026 Common Sense Media report, which surveyed more than 1,000 children ages 9 to 17, found that many were already treating AI as a confidant. An earlier 2025 study found that nearly three-quarters of teens had used AI companions, with a third choosing chatbots over humans for serious conversations. The nonprofit has warned that such relationships can pose risks to minors, particularly around emotional attachment and dependency.

Apple’s announcement also follows the company’s expanded parental controls in iOS 27, which gave parents more control over their children’s apps, browsing and screen time. The update introduced features that let parents approve websites before their children visit them, set time limits for app categories and restrict access to potentially harmful content.

However, an “18-plus” rating doesn’t automatically mean everyone has to verify their age. The labels work with parental restrictions, while Apple may require users to confirm they’re adults before downloading those adult apps in certain regions.

So the new rules won’t necessarily stop teenagers from finding a digital soulmate. They should, however, make it harder for apps offering romantic AI relationships to slip past age-based content restrictions — regardless of whether a chatbot says you’re mature for your age.
